School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 3 of 304 F DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 4 of 304 Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Section 1: Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Overview of Comprehensive Education Planning Comprehensive Education Planning (CEP) is a structured, data-driven process that guides schools in assessing their needs, identifying areas for growth, setting strategic goals and implementing targeted actions to improve student outcomes. Collaboratively developed by the School Leadership Team (SLT) in accordance with the requirements of Chancellor’s Regulation A-655, the CEP meets New York State Education Department (NYSED) requirements under the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA) and aligns with NYC Public Schools’ (NYCPS) system-wide priorities. By engaging all stakeholders—school leaders, teachers, families, students and community members—schools can create a data-informed, equity- focused roadmap for student success and continuous improvement. Schools should focus their CEP on the five system-wide priorities below, connecting their planning to the data discussions and conversations with stakeholders held as part of the school’s needs assessment: Priority 1: All students learn to read well Priority 2: All students are physically and emotionally safe Priority 3: All students have a high-quality academic experience Priority 4: All students graduate college and career ready and have a strong plan and pathway to economic security Priority 5: All districts and schools are more inclusive and responsive for parents and families, including having more families choose NYC Public Schools The Education Planning Process The CEP follows an equity-driven, continuous improvement cycle, rooted in the system-wide priorities. This begins with a comprehensive needs assessment of the school’s most current data to identify priority needs and root causes. Next, the SLT establishes specific, measurable, attainable, relevant, and timely— SMART goal(s) aligned to each priority and determines targets to measure progress toward meeting the annual goals. The SLT and instructional leaders define key evidence-based key strategies, professional learning activities and parent, family and community empowerment efforts. They monitor the implementation of those strategies and activities and track progress through measurable targets. Then, the SLT aligns the school-based budget with human and instructional resources to support action plans. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 5 of 304 As schools set goals, develop key strategies, and plan action steps, they provide opportunities for all children to meet NYSED’s proficient and advanced levels of student academic achievement. These include evidence based instructional strategies that address the educational needs of historically underserved populations and tackle structural inequities. Schools indicate how the plan addresses the needs of Multilingual/English Language Learners (ML/ELL) through development of the school’s Language Allocation Policy (LAP) & Title III Application (if applicable) and the Language Translation and Interpretation (LTI) Plan, that are required as part of the CEP. Schools also address the needs of Students with Disabilities (SWD) through the High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities section of the plan. SLTs also develop a plan to improve attendance and reduce chronic absenteeism. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 6 of 304 Guidance on Conducting a Comprehensive Needs Assessment Directions: Conduct a comprehensive needs assessment for each Priority. The information below provides guidance for reviewing and analyzing student and school performance trends to uncover root causes for low student achievement. 1. Review Multiple Data Sources: Use the most current and available information including, but not limited to, the following sources of formative and summative data, as applicable: Qualitative and quantitative data, including enrollment and class size trends 2024-25 ESSA Accountability Data Instructional Leadership Framework (ILF), Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) Inventories, and NYC School Survey results Quality Review Reports, NYSED and NYCPS CSI or A/TSI Support Visits, Receivership Quarterly Reports, and Diagnostic Tool for School and District Effectiveness Framework (DTSDE) NYC School Quality Guide, Insight, New Visions, NWEA MAP and/or other formative assessment data, IEP Review Survey Data, Progress on IEP Goals Data, Program (SESIS-STARS Linkage) Reports, Mandated (Related) Services Reports, and NYCPS Tools for Understanding Your Data for student subgroup information 2. Analyze Data Trend(s) to Identify Areas of Strength and Need: Identify data trends by grade, cohort, and content area(s) to surface gaps and inequities. Compare data sets to identify instructional and school practices that are impacting student performance. Guiding questions to consider: What does the data reveal about improving and declining student performance trends within grades, cohorts, and student subgroups? What inequities are evident between highest and lowest performing student subgroups? Which subgroup(s) scored at Level 1 based on NYSED ESSA accountability indicators? What does the data suggest regarding the effectiveness (or ineffectiveness) of current school programs, practices, and/or strategies/initiatives? What areas may need to be strengthened? Are there any differences in student performance between program delivery models for ML/ELL and SWD? (e.g., for special education: special class, ICT, SETSS, related services only; for ML/ELL: Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) vs. Dual Language, etc.) How does delivery of instruction factor into data trends? What are the implications for teacher quality and professional learning? What inequities in special education referral and school disciplinary practices (e.g. suspensions, removals) are evident between student subgroups with IEPs? 3. Identify Root Cause(s): After analyzing the data, engage in a process for uncovering root causes or contributing factors for low student and school performance. Develop theories or hypotheses to explain DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 7 of 304 why these needs exist and why your school is getting its current outcomes. Root causes could be related to gaps and inequities in areas such as curriculum & instruction, school systems & structures, student support services, school climate & safety, and family & community engagement. Focus on the causes, rather than the symptoms. Use the 5 Whys Method or other methods for identifying root causes. Consider the questions below: What theories or hypotheses explain the current student and school outcomes? Are there root causes specific to certain subgroups, grades, cohorts, or content area(s)? Additional resources and supports are available below. Use this CEP Needs Assessment Worksheet to help your SLT and stakeholders identify appropriate data sources, data trends, root causes and priority needs. For guidance and best practices on engaging stakeholders in a comprehensive needs assessment and root cause analysis, see this deck. A current list of all CEP/Title I resources can be found on the iPlan Portal Public Resources Page. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 16 of 304 P. S. 94, The Henry Longfellow, is a K-5 elementary school located in Sunset Park, Brooklyn. We have a culturally and linguistically diverse student population of close to 1000 students of which 49% are English Language Learners, and 13% are students with disabilities. We offer Dual Language programs in Spanish and Chinese in grades K-5. Our dual language programs affirm student identity and are exposed to content skills as well culturally relevant curricula. PS 94 is a school that prioritizes social and emotional learning in our instructional practice and school culture in order ensure that our students are engaging in their academic studies with a growth mindset and the goal of inspiring a generation of future advocates for justice. We acknowledge that children learn best when their emotional needs are met and that conflicts or incidents will occur when those needs are not being met. ELA K-5 Edwards-Orton This year, our school is making a significant investment in (Phonemic Gillingham foundational literacy by enhancing phonics instruction across awareness selected classrooms in grades K-5. Recognizing the critical role and that early reading skills play in long-term academic success, we Phonics) are proud to announce the integration of the Edwards-Orton Gillingham (EOG) approach into our core literacy framework. The Edwards-Orton Gillingham method is a research-based, multisensory instructional approach to teaching phonics and word recognition. Rooted in the original Orton-Gillingham principles, the EOG method combines direct, explicit, and systematic instruction in phonemic awareness, decoding, encoding, and fluency. It is particularly effective for diverse learners, including students with reading difficulties such as dyslexia, but benefits all students by providing a strong and structured foundation in the English language. This year, selected classroom teachers in grades K-5 will receive specialized professional development in the EOG approach. The training emphasizes how to implement multisensory phonics techniques in a way that aligns with both Tier 1 (core classroom instruction) and Tier 2 (targeted small group interventions) of our Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). Tier 1 Implementation: In Tier 1, the EOG method will be integrated into daily whole-class literacy blocks. Teachers will use structured lesson routines and multisensory activities that engage visual, auditory, and kinesthetic pathways to help all students master foundational reading skills. This ensures consistency, continuity, and fidelity across classrooms while addressing the diverse needs of learners within the general education setting. Tier 2 Implementation: For students DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 22 of 304 requiring additional support, the EOG approach will also be used in Tier 2 small-group interventions. These sessions will be more intensive and individualized, reinforcing skills taught in the core program and closing learning gaps through focused practice and corrective feedback. This dual-layer implementation will ensure that phonics instruction is not only consistent and high-quality across classrooms, but also responsive to the varying needs of our learners. We believe that investing in the Edwards-Orton Gillingham approach will strengthen early literacy outcomes and empower every student to become a confident, capable reader. We will monitor progress through ongoing formative assessments, classroom observations, and teacher feedback, ensuring that implementation remains strong and that student needs are met with fidelity. More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Attendance Progress Monitoring (Sept. 2025 – June 2026): - Monitor student attendance and punctuality to minimize learning disruptions Professional Development and Collaboration (Sept. 2025 – June 2026): - Engage teachers in structured PD through grade meetings and instructional coaching cycles, - Build teacher capacity to analyze ELA performance data, identify priority skills as well as zone of proximal development, plan small-group Tier 2 and Tier 3 small-group interventions with explicit modeling, visual supports, language scaffolds and evidence-based literacy routines rooted in the Science of Reading. - Coordinate common planning time with teachers and coaches to design ZPD-aligned small group instruction based on the school’s instructional focus - Plan professional development sessions to align differentiated instructional practices with HMH resources ELA Instructional Design: - Support teachers in sequencing ELA instruction logically, by breaking down chunking complex literary skills, and embedding phonemic awareness, decoding, fluency, vocabulary, and experiential learning tasks aligned to the Science of Reading - Unpack ELA academic language, explicitly teaching vocabulary in context, and structured academic talk routines and comprehension components. - Implement Orton-Gillingham instruction in selected classrooms, with a focus on phonics and morphology - Ensure daily implementation of Heggerty and Structured Literacy across all classrooms Content and Language Integration in ELA: - Use student language proficiency data to plan differentiated content and embed language objectives into ELA instruction (e.g., using sentence frames, vocabulary banks, and oral rehearsal strategies) across reading and writing tasks. - Incorporate scaffolds such as sentence frames, word banks, interactive read-alouds, and oral rehearsal strategies to support multilingual learners. Emphasize the integration of content knowledge (e.g., science and social studies themes) within literacy tasks to deepen conceptual understanding and engage diverse learners. Data-Driven MTSS Cycles: - Implement data trackers and formative assessments to monitor literacy growth in real time. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 39 of 304 - Use progress-monitoring data to refine instructional groups, increase intervention frequency or intensity, and align supports to the specific decoding, fluency, or comprehension needs of students—particularly those with IEPs or identified language needs. Ensure interventions align with SMART IEP goals and English language proficiency targets. Vertical and Cross-Content Planning: - Facilitate cross-grade and interdisciplinary collaboration to align ELA standards, vocabulary development, and academic language routines, and instructional strategies. - Develop performance tasks and experiential learning opportunities that embed reading, writing, and oral language opportunities tied to real-world themes, ensuring coherence and continuity of literacy instruction from grade to grade. - Facilitate weekly vertical team meetings for special education staff to address IEP goals and student needs - Support planning and implementation of specially designed instruction (SDI) as outlined in students’ IEPs Root Cause Addressed: Evidence indicates that students demonstrate a broad range of strengths and needs, particularly in language acquisition and content-specific skills. To address these disparities, it is essential to elevate Tier 2 small group instruction tailored to each student’s zone of proximal development. Strengthening Tier 2 instruction is key to closing learning gaps, reinforcing foundational skills, and accelerating progress for students below grade level. Data guiding these goals reflect both English language development for multilingual learners and skill-based proficiency for English-proficient students, including those at Levels 3 and 4—underscoring the need for both targeted remediation and academic enrichment. PS 94 has identified a critical need to strengthen instructional practices that build students’ phonological and phonemic awareness—key metalinguistic skills foundational to early reading development and decoding success. While this represents only one component of the broader Science of Reading, explicit instruction in these areas remains essential for all students, particularly those in Grades 3–5 who continue to struggle with word recognition, decoding accuracy, and reading fluency. Current data reveal persistent gaps in students' ability to recognize, manipulate, and apply sound-symbol correspondences and sound patterns of the English language. These gaps are evident in areas such as letter-sound correspondence, phonograms, CVC words, blends, digraphs, high-frequency words, and spelling. To address these challenges, there is a pressing need for explicit, systematic, and metalinguistically informed instruction during the ELA block and in small-group settings. Instruction must focus on isolating, blending, segmenting, and manipulating both large units of language and individual phonemes to support fluency, accuracy, and decoding. Strengthening these skills—especially for upper elementary students who may have missed foundational instruction—is vital to unlocking complex texts and closing long standing reading gaps. PS 94 has identified a critical need to elevate Tier 2 instruction through targeted, skill-based small group. There is a need for explicit instruction in vocabulary, fluency, reading comprehension strategies, grammar, and writing craft. Equally important is the effective use of ongoing progress monitoring to guide instructional decision-making. Frequent and systematic data collection is essential to inform the design of responsive, Tier 2 interventions and to ensure that instruction is tailored to students’ evolving needs. This continuous cycle of assessment, analysis, and targeted instruction is foundational to accelerating student growth and closing skill gaps. While PS 94 has established foundational practices to support Entering and Emerging English Language Learners, there remains a need for greater consistency and intentionality in the use of language acquisition data to inform instruction, create opportunities for purposeful academic language development alongside content mastery. Teachers are incorporating scaffolds and differentiated strategies, but there is still room to deepen expertise in applying second language acquisition theory to daily instruction. Additionally, while oral language opportunities and DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 40 of 304 some progress monitoring tools are in use, their integration across content areas and small group instruction is inconsistent. Strengthening these practices through strategic, schoolwide efforts will ensure instruction is responsive, language-rich, and fully aligned to the evolving needs of multilingual learners. Although PS 94 provides small group instruction for English Proficient learners, there is a continued need to strengthen educators’ understanding of how to deliver targeted, skill-based instruction that leverages students’ language strengths in ways that are both intentional and rigorous. EP students possess full command of English, allowing them to access and manipulate language to engage in deeper meaning-making. Instruction should therefore emphasize academic discourse, vocabulary, figurative language, and advanced comprehension strategies rather than foundational language development. While teachers are using academic data to form groups, instructional planning does not always align with individual skill-based needs or capitalize on students’ verbal and written fluency to deepen analysis, critical thinking, and metacognitive reflection. These learners benefit from complex, text-based tasks that promote synthesis, evaluation, and argumentation, as well as instruction that builds their capacity to analyze genre, style, author’s intent, and text structure. However, instructional adjustments are not consistently driven by real-time progress monitoring, which limits responsiveness to students’ evolving academic needs. There is a need for more strategic use of formative assessments and student work analysis to inform instruction that advances both foundational literacy and higher-order thinking skills. Elevating teacher expertise in using language strengths to design differentiated enrichment opportunities will ensure English Proficient learners continue to make meaningful academic gains across all content areas. Action Steps Professional Learning Cycles: Teachers will engage in ongoing professional development through grade meetings, instructional coaching, content area departments, ENL department sessions, and HMH and OG led training. Collaborative Instructional Planning: ENL staff, administrators, and ELA coaches will meet weekly to analyze literacy data, identify priority skills, and design learning experiences that include explicit instruction, differentiated scaffolds, and progress monitoring tools for all learners. Integration of Literacy Across Content Areas: Content area departments will collaborate with administrators and coaches to develop grade-level instructional maps that integrate literacy skills with content knowledge through experiential learning. Targeted Intervention Development: Grade teams, administrators, and ELA coaches will design small-group interventions focused on research-based practices to strengthen phonological and phonemic awareness. MTSS Implementation and Monitoring: The MTSS team—including administrators, coaches, ENL and content area staff, and the SEL coach—will meet weekly to monitor instructional cycles of support and track measurable outcomes in student progress and instructional practice. The following structures will be implemented to assess impact on instruction, student learning, and school culture: - Monthly PBIS and SEL Leadership Meetings: Review OSYD Portal incident data, behavior referral trends (OORS entries by time/location/infraction), and class-specific patterns to guide targeted Tier 1 and Tier 2 interventions. - SEL Implementation Walkthroughs (K–5): Conduct monthly look-fors using an SEL-aligned observation tool to assess use of CASEL-aligned strategies in both classroom and non-instructional settings (e.g., lunchroom, recess, hallway transitions). - Midday Supervision Logs & Behavior Tracking Sheets: School aides and SEL support staff will complete weekly supervision reflection logs to document student interactions, restorative practices used, and incidents managed. - Student Voice & Belonging Surveys Administer SEL surveys twice per year (K–2 and 3–5 versions) to measure growth in students’ sense of safety, empathy, self-regulation, and peer connection. - Restorative Practices Logbook & Mediation Tracker: Monitor frequency, type, and outcomes of peer mediation and restorative circles facilitated during lunch periods and class meetings. - Professional Learning Reflections: Track staff participation and feedback on trauma-informed care, de-escalation DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 59 of 304 training, and culturally responsive SEL implementation, with exit tickets and follow-up pulse checks. - Progress Reporting Tool (PRT): All implementation progress will be documented and tracked through the iPlan Portal’s Progress Reporting Tool during designated check-in cycles. June 2026 (Grades K-2:42% and Grades 3-5: 23%) of all students scoring at grade or above grade level (level 3 & level 4) DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 76 of 304 Priority 3 Action Plan Directions: Identify the key strategy and progressive action steps your school will take to address the specific priority needs and root causes identified for this goal(s). More than one strategy can be indicated if multiple and/or differentiated needs for this priority have been identified. Key strategies should be key levers for success and may represent something that is new to the school. Action steps to operationalize key strategies should focus on new activities or initiatives and/or modifications to current initiatives (e.g., expanded to reach a wider audience or refined for greater impact on students). Note: Routine activities your school does every year should not be included. Key Strategy 1 (Required): Attendance Progress Monitoring: - Monitor student attendance and punctuality to minimize learning disruptions Professional Development and Collaboration (Sept. 2025 – June 2026): - Engage teachers in structured PD through grade meetings and instructional coaching cycles, - Build teacher capacity to analyze math performance data, identify priority skills as well as zone of proximal development, plan small-group Tier 2/3 interventions with explicit modeling, visual supports, and language scaffolds. - Coordinate common planning time with teachers and coaches to design ZPD-aligned small group instruction based on the school’s instructional focus - Plan professional development sessions to align differentiated instructional practices with EM2 resources Mathematics Instructional Design: - Support teachers in sequencing math instruction logically, chunking complex skills, and incorporating hands-on and experiential learning tasks aligned to the math learning continuum. - Unpack math language and vocabulary, and explicitly teach problem-solving strategies using visuals, manipulatives, and structured academic talk routines. - Plan professional development sessions to align differentiated instructional practices with EM2 resources Content and Language Integration in Math: - Use student language proficiency data to plan differentiated content and embed language objectives into math instruction (e.g., using sentence frames, math-specific vocabulary banks, and oral rehearsal strategies). - Leverage technology and math tools (e.g., virtual manipulatives, digital math journals) to deepen conceptual understanding and engage diverse learners. Data-Driven MTSS Cycles: - Implement data trackers and formative assessments to monitor student progress in real time. - Use data to adjust instruction and increase frequency and intensity of interventions for students not making adequate growth, especially those with IEPs or language development needs. - Align instruction to SMART IEP goals where applicable, ensuring instruction meets both mathematical and language development targets. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 77 of 304 Vertical and Cross-Content Planning: - Facilitate cross-grade and interdisciplinary collaboration to align math content knowledge, academic language, and instructional strategies. - Develop performance tasks and experiential learning opportunities that integrate mathematical thinking with real- world problem solving. - Facilitate weekly vertical team meetings for special education staff to address IEP goals and student needs - Support planning and implementation of specially designed instruction (SDI) as outlined in students’ IEPs Root Cause Addressed: Priority 1 Root Cause: PS 94 has identified an ongoing challenge in effectively integrating language development within mathematics instruction. Many teachers, while confident in delivering math content, may not view themselves as language instructors—yet they are responsible for ensuring all students, including multilingual learners and students with disabilities, can access and engage with rigorous mathematical concepts. Research confirms that mathematics texts are linguistically dense, often embedding multiple concepts per sentence or paragraph, and conveyed through symbolic and abstract language. As a result, the oral and written communication necessary for math understanding is often underemphasized. Data trends continue to demonstrate the need for explicit instruction that merges literacy and mathematical thinking, particularly in areas such as language acquisition, building background knowledge, increasing student language production, and teaching academic vocabulary. To address this, there is a clear need to elevate Tier 2 small group, skill-based instruction that targets each student’s zone of proximal development. Effective Tier 2 instruction requires systematic data analysis, explicit delivery, differentiated scaffolds, accurate and specific feedback, and regular progress monitoring using data trackers. These practices not only support foundational skills but also accelerate student growth in content-specific areas. Furthermore, for students with other abilities, Specially Designed Instruction (SDI) must be embedded within Tier 2/3 small group instruction. SDI involves adapting content, methodology, or instructional delivery to meet individual student needs as outlined in their IEP goals. It must be intentional, systematic, and responsive—ensuring meaningful access to the general curriculum through small group or 1:1 instruction. Priority 2 Root Cause: Substantial research shows that collaborative learning promotes active learning, critical thinking, communication skills, social development, a positive learning environment, deeper understanding of concepts, and preparation for real-life situations. For example, a 2014 NCTM study found that mathematical conversations and discourse among students—at all grade and ability levels—helps build a shared understanding of mathematical ideas. Problem-based learning offers a powerful approach to collaborative learning in math. The focus is teachers emphasizing the importance of the process of getting to the answer, encouraging the sharing of “rough draft ideas” that students can develop together. Ultimately the process is for students to reflect on what they’re doing rather than just report what they did. The Math Carousel is a cooperative learning practice and the goal is to engage students to express their mathematical reasoning and really think about the demands of an Exemplar task through deep discussions before writing their mathematical thinking. Students sustain discussions in small groups while the teacher circulates and facilitates the discussions. The Carousel allows students to comment, discuss and/or respond (verbally and/or in writing) to probing questions, statements, topics, themes that are posted by the teacher. Ultimately students unpack the rigorous demands of a task with their peers (group) and engage in hands-on experiences using deliberate scaffolds and mathematical tools as needed. In addition, explicit language scaffolds are accessible to all learners to DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 78 of 304 be successful in the job they are expected to do. Priority 3 Root Cause: Students who learn only mathematical facts, definitions, rules, and procedures may perform well on large-scale tests that address these relatively easy-to-score elements of mathematics. But many of these same students later find that they cannot use what they know when they encounter any problem or situation they haven’t specifically learned how to solve. We are aware of the preparation of students who cannot apply what they’ve learned, we are also mindful we cannot continue to cling to the old notion that mathematics consists primarily of a checklist of knowledge and skills. In reviewing the math data from previous years, it is noted subgroups such as MLLs and SWOAs consistently underperform grade level than peers. This data inquires us to think about: “how can math habits of mind and SEL be effectively integrated into grade-level mathematics instruction? Are all students equally comfortable contributing? Do all students have equal agency?” When educators recognize that math habits are the basis for mathematics instruction and learning, they: –enhance the ways students produce knowledge rather than how they merely reproduce it. –students learn how to develop a critical stance with their work: inquiring, editing, thinking flexibly, and learning from another person’s perspective. The critical attribute of an intellectual individual is not only having information but also knowing how to act on it. These learning experiences bring a new and unique understanding and prepare students for the math they experience in their everyday lives. In our classroom, students cannot do math in isolation. Effective math instruction are planned experiences that include: -growth mindset -healthy collaboration and conflict resolution -coping with frustration when challenged by mathematical concepts/skills. When math habits and SEL are seen as interwoven vital components of mathematics learning, students will increase academic achievement, improve attitudes and behaviors, and reduce emotional distress. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 145 of 304 Action Steps: Indicate what your school will do specifically to implement each strategy to achieve this annual goal. Include both student-facing activities and professional learning for staff. Be sure to indicate how action steps will be introduced and rolled out. Action Steps Ongoing Professional Learning Structures Teachers will participate in continuous professional development through grade meetings, instructional coaching cycles, and supported trainings focused on best practices in literacy and mathematics instruction. The Professional Learning Sessions will be internal as well as external such as district and central professional learning focused on SDI, IEP writing, progress monitoring. Integrated Literacy and Math Planning (Weekly) Administrators, instructional coaches and ENL providers will collaborate weekly to analyze student literacy and mathematics data, identify priority skills, and design learning experiences with: Explicit instruction Differentiated content and language scaffolds Data trackers for progress monitoring Teachers will examine the intersection of language and content, recognizing the critical role language plays in developing and expressing both literacy and mathematical understanding. MTSS Implementation and Progress Monitoring (bi-weekly) The MTSS team instructional coaches, MTSS providers and administration will meet bi-weekly to document and review instructional cycles of support. These meetings will focus on aligning instruction to measurable student and teacher outcomes and adjusting intervention plans as needed. Student Support Case Review (Bi-Weekly) The MTSS Team such as instructional coaches, ENL providers, and classroom teachers will collaborate bi-weekly to review individual student cases, analyze progress monitoring data, and align MTSS supports to ensure students receive appropriate interventions in a timely manner. Attendance and Punctuality Monitoring (Ongoing) Student attendance and punctuality will be closely monitored to reduce learning disruptions and ensure consistent access to instruction and interventions. Deep Engagement with IEPs to Support SDI (Ongoing) Teachers will engage in regular review and discussion of students’ Individualized Education Programs (IEPs) to fully understand each student’s unique learning needs and ensure the effective implementation of Specially Designed Instruction (SDI) aligned to IEP goals and accommodations. Key Strategy 2 (Optional): Conduct IEP meetings within specified compliance dates Develop standards-aligned, rigorous annual IEP goals that are directly connected to each student’s unique learning needs and guide the delivery of Specially Designed Instruction (SDI). Ensure the timely and consistent delivery of all programs and services as mandated in each student’s Individualized Education Program (IEP). Action Steps The Assistant Principal and Centrally Funded Intervention Teacher will continue to develop and maintain an IEP Meeting Calendar—including Annual Reviews, M3Y, Re-Evaluations, Initials, and T5 meetings—to ensure that all IEP meetings are scheduled at least three weeks in advance of their compliance due dates. This proactive approach supports timely communication with families, service providers, and team members, ensuring compliance and quality planning. The Assistant Principal overseeing special education services will meet regularly with special education teachers to provide professional development and coaching on developing SMART, standards-aligned IEP goals. These meetings will also focus on aligning Specially Designed Instruction (SDI) to students' individualized goals and ensuring that effective progress monitoring systems are in place to track growth, inform instructional adjustments, and support meaningful student outcomes. The Assistant Principal will conduct weekly reviews of the Program Recommended Services Report and Service Records Report to ensure that all students are receiving the programs and services mandated on their IEPs, SESIS encounter attendance is entered and in alignment with compliance requirements. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 150 of 304 High Quality Inclusive & Accessible Learning for Students with Disabilities Budget and Resources Budget and Resources Directions: Indicate available and anticipated resources that will be leveraged to achieve this annual goal and implement this action plan, including human resources, instructional resources (e.g., curricula, programs), schedule adjustments, other. Use N/A if not applicable and/or indicate other resource needs. Schools are encouraged to think critically and strategically when considering resources that can support progress towards CEP goals for students with IEPs. This includes utilizing Title I funding for at-risk speech and/or counseling services, all-staff training in supporting students with autism, sensory materials, summer institute geared at the development of executive functioning skills, and/or inclusion coaching support. Schools may also consider utilizing Title II funds to reduce the ratio of students with IEPs to typically developing peers in ICT classes. The Multi-tiered Systems of Support (MTSS) framework, organizes supports into three tiers: Tier 1 provides schoolwide, high-quality programming for all students, including SWDs; Tier 2 offers data informed small-group interventions for at-promise students; and Tier 3 delivers intensive, individualized support for students with persistent needs. This facilitates the strategic planning and necessary resource alignment to ensure their school community appropriately and adequately serves all students well, in partnership with students and families. MTSS identifies the academic support services to be provided and ensures that students receive Response to Intervention (RtI) and Positive Behavioral Interventions and Support (PBIS) strategies as needed. See NYSED’s memo to view the criteria for determining AIS services. The Supportive Environment Framework (SEF) offers a wide range of evidence-based Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions and supports for student social-emotional, behavioral, physical and mental wellness, with particular attention to the unique needs of students in Temporary Housing and/or Foster Care, who may face additional challenges related to school stability, trauma and access to educational resources. A culturally responsive-sustaining approach to intervention embeds culturally relevant texts and historical narratives into literacy and social studies interventions, ensuring students receive the support that reflects their identities and lived experiences, increasing engagement and achievement. Use the charts below and a Multi-Tiered System of Support (MTSS) to match academic, social/emotional, behavioral and instructional resources with student needs to provide targeted support for all learners, especially at-risk students who experience difficulty meeting state standards, including those living in Temporary Housing and/or residing in Foster Care placements. Indicate specialized materials, technology- based tools and/or extra instruction, including Expanded Learning Time that supplement school-wide systems & supports and/or classroom level supports. Ensure that interventions address the unique social- emotional, behavioral and academic challenges faced by culturally and linguistically diverse students, as well as those impacted by housing instability and child welfare system involvement. At P.S. 94, we believe that strong home-school partnerships are essential to student success. The essential components of our Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) are first introduced to families during our annual Curriculum Night and then reinforced during our initial Parent-Teacher Conferences. Throughout the year, as students are identified for Tier 2 or Tier 3 supports, families will receive additional information about the MTSS process and what it entails. We ensure that parents are fully informed about the supports and progress monitoring tools being used. Communication will take place in the family's preferred language and focus on student work, attendance, and specific academic or social-emotional goals. Notices and permission slips will be sent home to secure consent for services and ongoing communication. Describe how families receive updates on the progress of their child(ren) who receive Tier 2 targeted, or Tier 3 intensive interventions. Families of students receiving Tier 2 or Tier 3 interventions are engaged through regular updates from the classroom teacher, the service provider, and/or an instructional coach. These updates include the use of a progress monitoring tool aligned to specific goals, checklists of targeted skills, and samples of student work that demonstrate growth over time. Progress is discussed during one-on-one meetings, which can be scheduled in person or remotely based on family preference. We ensure communication is accessible and consistent, and we include both academic and behavioral indicators such as attendance, participation, and student engagement. Describe how families are involved during decision making regarding the progress of students receiving Tier 3 intensive intervention. At P.S. 94, families are considered active partners in the MTSS decision-making process—especially at the Tier 3 level. Parents provide consent before services begin and are encouraged to observe sessions, ask questions, and share input. This is an open and collaborative process, and our staff—including teachers, coaches, and related service providers—remain available for follow-up conversations and progress reviews. Our doors are always open, and we are committed to keeping families informed and engaged in their child’s learning journey through individualized meetings, updates in their home language, and meaningful discussions centered around data, student work, and next steps. STH: 215 Double up: 210 Shelter: 3 Hotel: 2 After a careful analysis of historical demographic, attendance and achievement data for your STH population, what trends do you notice, and how can these trends inform the services and support provided for this population? Analysis of PS 94K’s 2024–2026 STH data reveals that while total enrollment has decreased, the number of students in temporary housing has risen from 210 to 215—now representing 28% of the school population. Attendance data show a persistent 26% chronic absenteeism rate, with many students hovering just below the 90% threshold, highlighting the need for consistent outreach and stability supports. Academic data show steady growth: reading proficiency rose from 36% to 42% and math from 41% to 48%, demonstrating the effectiveness of Orton-Gillingham and small-group instruction. However, early literacy and attendance remain key barriers. In response, PS 94K will strengthen attendance mentoring, expand early intervention in reading, extend math enrichment programs, and deepen community partnerships to address both academic and social-emotional needs. These data-driven supports aim to ensure that all students in temporary housing experience academic continuity, emotional safety, and equitable access to learning. How many students in foster care are currently enrolled at your school? Provide a detailed breakdown of the social-emotional and academic supports available to them. As of this academic year, P.S. 94 has 4 students in foster care. We view every student in our care as a whole child— one who deserves safety, dignity, and the opportunity to thrive academically, socially, and emotionally. Social-Emotional Supports: Foster care students are prioritized for real-time SEL support through our in-house SEL coaches and guidance counselors, including check-ins, small group sessions, and restorative practices. We facilitate direct connections to internal and external mental health services, including referrals to licensed clinicians via our partnerships with Together Growing Strong, Center for Family Life, and NYU Langone’s BASIS Program. Foster care students have priority access to school-based mental wellness resources and are continuously monitored for signs of social-emotional distress so that supports can be mobilized immediately. Academic Supports: Students are assessed using Acadience (K–2) and MAP Growth (3–5) within their first two weeks to determine current performance levels. Based on data, they are placed into Tier 2 or Tier 3 small group instruction for targeted support in reading, math, or foundational skills, including instruction via the Edwards-Orton Gillingham phonics approach and Eureka Math Squared. Instructional supports include afterschool and Saturday programs, as well as ongoing 1:1 and small group conferring with coaches and interventionists. Basic Needs Support: All foster care students are automatically connected to our school’s food pantry, clothing boutique, and free laundry facility. They are issued learning devices and hotspots, if needed, and provided school supplies, hygiene kits, and uniforms. These supports are offered without delay to ensure students’ essential needs are met so they can engage fully in learning. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 159 of 304 What targeted supports are in place for students in foster care, including those newly transitioning into the foster care system? How does your school ensure these students receive the necessary interventions to promote stability and success? Every student in foster care receives an individualized transition plan developed in partnership with caregivers, teachers, counselors, and service providers. We recognize that transitions can be disorienting, so we respond with urgency, compassion, and coordination. Targeted Supports Include: Real-time access to mental health support, including immediate intake by our counseling staff and fast-tracked referrals to external providers. A designated school-based Success Mentor assigned to each new student to support daily routines, check in on emotional well-being, and help the student navigate the school day. Academic screening (Acadience/MAP Growth) within the first two weeks to ensure timely placement into intervention groups aligned to the student’s Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD). Enrollment in afterschool academic programs and Saturday enrichment when appropriate, with outreach to caregivers to facilitate consent and participation. Immediate access to basic needs resources, including food, clean clothing, laundry services, and devices for schoolwork and communication. Caregiver Communication and Consent: Parents or guardians (including foster parents and caseworkers) receive notices and permission slips for any targeted interventions. Progress and participation updates are provided in the caregiver’s preferred language and through their preferred method (in-person, phone, or virtual). After analyzing historical demographic, attendance, and achievement data for students in foster care, what trends have emerged? How can these insights be used to improve the services and support provided to this population? Our review of multi-year data shows several recurring patterns for students in foster care, underscoring the impact of instability and trauma—but also pointing to clear action steps. Emerging Trends: Attendance concerns, particularly among newly transitioned students, which correlate with housing or placement changes. Reading and math gaps, especially in decoding, fluency, and vocabulary acquisition. Increased behavioral referrals, often tied to emotional regulation or social adaptation challenges. Responsive Actions and Continuous Improvement: We have enhanced our early identification systems to flag attendance, academic performance, and SEL needs in real time. Data now triggers automatic referral to the MTSS team, where academic and SEL supports are co-designed to provide coordinated interventions. These students are also prioritized for mental health referrals, mentorship, and small group counseling. All foster care students are guaranteed access to wraparound supports including food, clothing, laundry, and device access, ensuring their basic needs do not become barriers to success. Our teams meet regularly to review progress, using updated data to refine and strengthen supports as students transition and grow. What specific strategies does your school implement to ensure students in foster care, particularly those in transitional grades, receive targeted Social-Emotional Learning (SEL) support? How do these DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 160 of 304 strategies help address academic and behavioral challenges? We believe SEL must be more than a program—it must be a way of being. At P.S. 94, we embed SEL into instruction, relationships, and routines, ensuring every child, especially those in foster care, is seen, heard, and valued. Targeted SEL Strategies for Transitional Grades (2nd and 5th): SEL Circles and Mentorship: Foster care students are invited to join SEL reflection circles facilitated by counselors and coaches, and are paired with trusted peer mentors and adult staff. Trauma-Informed Care: All staff receive training in trauma-responsive teaching, and classroom practices include de- escalation, conflict resolution, and co-regulation strategies. Real-Time Response: Behavioral incidents are met with immediate check-ins from counselors or the SEL coach, with a goal of repair and learning—not punishment. Affirmation and Belonging: SEL lessons are culturally responsive and identity-affirming, helping students navigate identity, trust, and resilience. Civic Voice and Agency: Students in foster care are encouraged to participate in student-led initiatives such as the Student Council, Youth Equity Congress, and The Rise Chronicle newspaper to develop agency and self-advocacy. Whole-Child Focus: These strategies foster academic stability by addressing emotional well-being. When students feel safe and connected, they engage more fully in learning, participate more confidently in class, and build positive relationships with peers and staff. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 163 of 304 Section 7: Parent and Family Engagement Policy and School-Parent Compact Section 7: Parent and Family Engagement Policy and School-Parent Compact Parent and Family Engagement Policy Directions: The Parent and Family Engagement Policy should describe how your school will plan and implement effective parent and family engagement activities and/or strategies to improve student academic achievement and school performance. The School-Parent Compact (SPC) is a component of the Parent and Family Engagement Policy (PFEP) that outlines how parents, the entire school staff, and students will share this responsibility. The activities and/or strategies included in your school's Parent and Family Engagement Policy should align with current Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP) goals for improving student achievement and should be developed or updated with input and feedback from parent leaders and the Parent Coordinator. In addition, if the school community will be engaged in central parent engagement initiatives, such as Parent Academy, which will provide training for school communities to help strengthen family-school partnerships, please be sure to include these activities in the school's policy. In Title I schools, the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) represents the Title I parent community in supporting parent and family engagement. The Title I PAC Chairperson will serve in a consultative role with the SLT representing the school’s Title I parents. Schools are encouraged to use the sample Parent and Family Engagement and SPC templates below (which meet federal Title I parent and family engagement requirements) as guidance for updating the school's current policy. Parent and Family Engagement Policy Educational research shows a positive correlation between effective parental engagement and student achievement. The overall aim of this policy is to develop a parent and family engagement policy that will establish expectations and objectives for meaningful parent and family engagement in the school community. PS 94, in compliance with Section 1116 of Title I, Part A of the Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), is responsible for creating and implementing a parent and family engagement policy to strengthen the connection and support of student achievement between the school and the families. The school's policy is designed to keep parents informed by actively involving them in planning and decision-making in support of the education of their children. Parents are encouraged to actively participate on the School Leadership Team, Parent Association (or Parent-Teacher Association), and Title I Parent Advisory Council as trained volunteers and welcomed members of the school community. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 169 of 304 Title I Program Information Title I Program Information Part 1: Title I Program Type Title I Requirements and Strengthening Title I Parent Involvement: Under USDOE’s Every Student Succeeds Act (ESSA), Title I schools are required to address professional learning and parent and family engagement in the Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP). To strengthen parent leadership in Title I schools, and increase the involvement of all parents of Title I eligible children in improving students’ academic outcomes, all Title I schools are required to establish a Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) that will serve as the consultative and representative body for all Title I parents in the school to provide voice into the spending of Title I funds. The SLT is responsible for facilitating consultation with the Title I Parent Advisory Council regarding the joint development of the CEP, Parent and Family Engagement Policy, School Parent Compact (SPC) and the use of the 1% parent involvement set-aside in Title I schools. The Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC) Chairperson (or alternate) is expected to attend all regular meetings of the School Leadership Team (SLT). Title I parents, as represented by the Title I PAC Chairperson, must be consulted regarding the use of the Title I 1% set-aside for parent and family engagement. The Title I 1% set-aside funding is used to supplement parent and family engagement activities as described in the school’s Parent and Family Engagement Policy and must be aligned with student achievement goals as outlined in the CEP. For additional guidance on the Title I Parent Advisory Council (PAC), go to the Protocols and Timeline for Title I Parent and Family Engagement Activities document. Explanation/Background: Title I Schoolwide Program schools are expected to use the flexibility available to them to integrate services and programs with the aim of enhancing the entire educational program and helping all students reach proficient and advanced levels of achievement. In addition to coordinating and integrating services, Schoolwide Program schools may combine most Federal, State and local funds to provide those services. By consolidating funds from Federal, State, and local sources, a Schoolwide Program school can address its needs using all its available resources. This gives a school more flexibility in how it uses available resources to meet the identified needs of all its students. Consolidating funds in a Schoolwide Program means that a school treats the funds it is consolidating like they are a single “pool” of funds. In other words, the funds from the contributing programs in the school lose their individual identity and the school has one flexible pool of funds. The school uses funds from this consolidated Schoolwide pool to support any activity of the Schoolwide Program without regard to which program contributed the specific funds used for a particular activity – so long as the use of funds meets the intent and purposes of the programs from which the funds originate. To consolidate funding in a Schoolwide Program, the school does not literally need to combine funds in a single account or pool with its own accounting code. Rather, the word “pool” is used conceptually to convey that a Schoolwide Program school has the use of all consolidated funds available to it for the dedicated function of operating a Schoolwide Program without regard to the identity of those funds. Consolidating Federal funds in a Schoolwide Program has the following additional advantages: Consolidating Federal funds eases the requirements for accounting for funds from each specific program separately, because a Schoolwide school is not required to distinguish among funds received from different sources when accounting for their use. A school that consolidates Federal funds in its Schoolwide Program is not required to meet most of the statutory and regulatory requirements of the specific Federal programs included in the consolidation (e.g., semi-annual time and effort reporting for Title I). However, the school must ensure that it meets the intent and purposes of the Federal programs included in the consolidation so that the needs of the intended beneficiaries are met. 2. The intent and purposes of the Federal programs indicated on the chart above (Part 4c of this section) are as follows: Title I, Part A – Schoolwide Programs: To upgrade the entire educational program in the school in order to improve the academic achievement of all students, particularly the lowest-achieving students. This includes provision of services for Students in Temporary Housing (STH). Title I 1003(a), School Improvement: Comprehensive Support and Improvement (CSI), Additional Targeted Support and Improvement (ATSI) and Targeted Support and Improvement (TSI) Funding: DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 175 of 304 Support implementation of school improvement plans that aims to improve instruction and address the identified needs Title II, Part A: Supplementary funding to improve student academic achievement by reducing class size in grades K, 1, 2, and 3, with an emphasis on grades with an average register greater than 20. If space is not available to form additional classes, funds may support push-in teacher(s) to supplement the instructional program. For schools that choose to reduce class size, priority must be given to reducing class size in general education settings and must demonstrate the following: Reduce class sizes to 15-18 students or fewer (including the use of co-teaching and floating teachers that bring the student to teacher ratio to one teacher per 15-18 students); Be accompanied by a rigorous curriculum, effective teachers, and a sustained, job-embedded professional development plan. Title III, Part A: To help ensure that children with limited English proficiency become proficient in English, develop high academic attainment in English, and meet the same challenging State academic content and achievement standards in the core academic subjects that all other children are expected to meet. Another purpose of this program is to increase the capacity of schools to establish, implement and sustain high-quality language instruction programs and English language development programs that assist schools in effectively teaching students with limited English proficiency. Title III, Part A is also designed to promote the participation of parents and communities of limited English proficient children in English language instruction programs. Title III Immigrant: Supplementary and enhanced services to immigrant students, in the areas of English language acquisition and content area achievement. Immigrant children and youth are defined as individuals who are aged 3 through 21, have not been attending school in any one or more states for more than three (3) full academic years, and were not born in any state. “State” means the 50 states of the United States, the District of Columbia, and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. Children born to U.S. citizens abroad, the U.S. Virgin Islands, or any other U.S. territory that is not D.C. or Puerto Rico are to be considered immigrants. Title IV, Part A: Title IV funds are for schools that are among those with the greatest needs, have the highest numbers of students from low-income families, are identified for CS, ATSI or TSI, or are identified as persistently dangerous public schools. Allowable uses of funds under well-rounded education, safety and health, and educational technology include direct services for students, professional development for teachers and administrators, and supplemental educational resources. School allocations have been provided for well-rounded education, social emotional learning, cultural responsiveness awareness, professional development to support implementation of technology and blended learning environments, and community coordinators in schools with high rates of students in temporary housing Students in Temporary Housing (STH): Schools must describe their use of Title I STH funds in their consolidated application. Each Community School is different and reflects the strengths and needs of its students, families, and local community. The most successful Community Schools share common features, which are integrated into the fabric of the school and drive student learning. These features are Collaborative Leadership and Practice, Family and Community Engagement, Expanded Learning Time (ELT), and Wellness and Integrated Supports. Embedded throughout the work is a focus on incorporating anti-racist practices and differentiating supports, including attendance supports, for special populations. Since all school operations and actions are in service of improving the academic and non-academic achievement of its students, the annual schoolwide goals should be informed by the students, their achievement, and the opportunities provided to them. The school should develop these goals after a realistic and comprehensive analysis of its data, paying specific attention to the achievement of its subgroups and disproportionality. Our partnership with the Chinese American Planning Council continues to strengthen and adapt to meet the evolving needs of the PS 94 school community. In the school year 2024-2025, we collaboratively launched a new needs assessment survey to gain fresh insights into family priorities. The survey was co-designed by teachers, administrators, family outreach staff, and the CPC director and was distributed in English, Chinese and Spanish through a variety of formats including online forms, printed copies, individual conversations, and during parent engagement events. Survey results revealed clear community needs. Families expressed strong interest in adult education classes focused on English language and computer skills, as well as after-school and enrichment programming in areas such as art, music, sports and technology. Families also voiced a desire for continued access to food assistance, mental health support, housing services, and legal or immigration resources. CPC plays a central role in addressing these needs through a collaborative planning model. CPC director regularly attends our monthly School Leadership Team meetings and participates in biweekly planning sessions with the Principal and Instructional Cabinet. These sessions focus on coordinating logistics for extended learning time, aligning available resources, reviewing family feedback, and planning for program delivery. CPC director also joins informal check-ins with school leaders to support ongoing alignment between programming and family needs. Together, we have taken action based on this shared leadership approach. We have expanded the PS 94 Food Pantry and Clothing Boutique, developed adult classes in English and technology, provided workshops to support parents’ SEL development, and a family resource room with childcare and computer access to make school events and services more inclusive. Planning is underway for a community bulletin board to improve family connection and access to information. Family and Community Engagement: Describe how your Community School will develop authentic school-family partnerships, and create the conditions for families and community members to participate in decision‐making, hold leadership roles that drive school change, and serve as partners in students’ holistic development. At PS 94, we believe that families are essential partners in student success and school transformation. The CPC director participates in key planning and leadership meetings to ensure that family needs and perspectives are consistently integrated into our academic programs and community initiatives. This includes monthly SLT meetings and ongoing planning sessions with the Principal and Instructional Cabinet. These collaborative spaces allow us to align programs and services with real-time family feedback and create inclusive opportunities for participation. We are deepening family leadership through school-based committees such as the Pantry and Clothing Boutique committee. Parents on these teams take the lead in decisions around food selection, culturally relevant offerings, and the organization distribution events. Their leadership ensures that the services we offer reflect the values, traditions, and lived experiences of the families we serve. The 2024-2025 family needs assessment survey confirmed that parents are eager for more opportunities to connect with one another and with the school. Many families expressed interest in adult education classes, community events, and bulletin boards to share resources and build connections. In response, we have increased family engagement through English language and computer classes and community building events. We are also using family surveys, informal conversations, and event feedback to guide our planning for school improvement, external partnerships, and community services. These tools support meaningful input and ensure that families help shape the vision and direction of our school. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 180 of 304 DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 181 of 304 Implementation of Community School Core Features & Components Wellness and Integrated Supports: Describe how your Community School will address out-of-school barriers to learning through partnerships with social and health service agencies and providers to help students attend school regularly and engage in learning through the provision of programs such as social emotional learning, conflict resolution training, and restorative justice. At PS 94, we are committed to addressing out-of-school barriers to learning by working closely with our community based organization partner, the Chinese American Planning Council, and other social and health service agencies. Together, we provide support that ensures students can attend school regularly, engage in learning, and thrive socially and emotionally. In the school year 2024-2025, we conducted a new family needs assessment survey to better understand what services are most needed by our community. Families shared that their top concerns remain consistent with prior years. They continue to view PS 94 as a safe and trusted space, and want their children to spend more time in our care through after school programs, summer enrichment, and wellness services. More than half of the respondents expressed interest in programs like after school support, food assistance, academic enrichment, and community building events. Parents also identified mental health, technology, English classes, and access to legal, housing, and medical support as essential resources for the community. In response to this feedback, we have continued to strengthen our integrated services. Our school-based team includes a social worker, school psychiatrist, bilingual guidance counselor, and SEL coach. This team supports students through one to one check ins, small group counseling, wellness programming, and SEL lessons embedded into the school day. We also use conflict resolution and restorative practices to help students develop positive relationships and resolve interpersonal challenges in a safe and respectful way. Our partnership with CPC expands these supports beyond the classroom. CPC operates a daily after school program for students in K-5 and runs a fifth grade leadership academy to prepare students for the transition to middle school. They also offer adult education classes in English and technology and host community events such as block parties and other services with partners like NYU Langone to promote health and wellness. CPC also helped to launch a family resource room to provide access to computers, application assistance, and childcare during family events. Based on the survey feedback, we are also planning to expand student activities in areas that families identified as high interest, such as art, music, dance, soccer and technology. These activities not only foster engagement and confidence but also contribute to emotional resilience and overall school connectedness. Every Student, Every Day: Describe how your Community School will work with CBO partners to meet weekly and use data from DOE systems and New Visions for Public Schools to monitor trends in attendance, assign personalized interventions–including Success Mentors–to chronically absent students, and draw on community resources for support with removing barriers to attendance. For support, you may access strategies here. At PS 94, we are committed to ensuring that every student is present, supported, and engaged in learning each day. While overall attendance continues to remain strong with daily rates typically above 90%, we remain focused on identifying and supporting students who are chronically absent or at risk of becoming chronically absent. We understand that consistent presence in school is essential for students to fully access instruction, develop social emotional skills and succeed alongside their peers. In addition to the existing taskforce of administrators, parent coordinators, guidance counselors, and SEL Instructional Coaches that are committed to ensuring our Students in Temporary Housing are receiving the supports and resources needed to overcome barriers to attendance, our DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 182 of 304 partnering CBO’s will ensure that none of our vulnerable Students in Temporary Housing fall through the cracks. Using daily and weekly attendance data, our parent coordinators will be in regular contact with any chronically absent students to determine the cause of their absence so that we may target our assistance with precision. We will make every effort to ensure that any barriers to attendance are overcome swiftly and with the help of our CBO, the root causes of chronic absenteeism will be addressed to the fullest extent. The funding received via the McKinney Vento Act will be allocated to students and their families based on an extensive needs assessment survey to determine the kinds of materials and resources each and every student needs. Each year a team of staff members call each individual family to determine what items they lack and if applicable, what sizes and amounts they need of each item. In the past we have purchased school supplies, clothing, toiletries, cleaning supplies and other household items. Our STH coordinators have also assigned Success Mentors from our body of out-of-class teachers, instructional coaches and staff to each student in temporary housing. We have designed programming supports in the form of wellness check-ins, small group activities, and after school enrichment which are implemented by our Success Mentors. These supports will further advance the goals of promoting steady attendance and academic engagement with our temporary housing students. We have built a range of services at PS 94 that support student and family needs beyond academics. Our Food Pantry and Clothing Boutique provide families with access to nutritious food and essential daily items. Students and families also have access to free laundry facilities on site, and we are continuing our partnership with NYU Langone to maintain and expand health services, including a school-based dental clinic. Insights from the 2024-2025 family needs assessment survey highlight how much our families value these supports. Families shared that the food pantry, after-school programming, and access to services have helped them send their children to school more consistently. They continue to view PS 94 as a safe, welcoming place where students and families are cared for and connected. Special Populations: Describe how your Community School will work with your partners to ensure students receive differentiated and culturally responsive supports. Describe how your Community School’s systems and structures will be optimized to work for all students, including students in temporary housing, Multilingual Learner/English Language Learner (ML/ELL), and Students with Disabilities. PS 94 is a school of linguistically, culturally, and ability diverse students. From the moment one enters the school building, there is an abundance of visible decorations that reflect the multitude of cultures in our school community as well as linguistically diverse signage that provide information to families, students, staff, and community members. Furthermore, the way in which we speak about these special populations is affirming and from a value added perspective. Our students are not English Language Learners, they are Multilingual Learners or Emergent Bilinguals; our students with IEPs are Students with Other Abilities, not Students with Disabilities. The terminology used to describe these students’ colors is how staff develops differentiated and culturally responsive supports and using positive terms will ensure that the supports we develop capitalize on their strengths in order to support their areas of need. If the school was unable to obtain a signature of an individual that participated in the development of the CEP, the school has written “Addendum Attached” and provided supplemental documentation to explain why the school was unable to obtain the individual’s signature. ☐ The CEP will be implemented no later than the beginning of the first day of regular student attendance. ☐ Professional development will be provided to teachers and school leaders that will fully support the strategic efforts described within this plan. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 185 of 304 Language Allocation Policy Outline Language Allocation Policy Outline Language Allocation Policy Outline 2025-26 Language Allocation Policy (LAP) This submission form supports schools with organizing a well-conceived school-based Language Allocation Policy (LAP) that describes high quality programs for Multilingual Learners/English Language Learners (MLs/ELLs). This is an annual plan of how your school will support the linguistic and academic needs of MLs/ELLs and must be completed yearly. The Language Allocation Policy (LAP) is a part of schools’ Comprehensive Education Plan (CEP). This is a living document. Any school-wide changes involving the instruction of MLs/ELLs, such as the addition or expansion of a Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) or Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) program, should be updated in this form. Agenda, minutes of LAP meetings, and LAP team members’ attendance at meetings should be kept readily available on file in the school with all other ML/ELL critical documents. When preparing your school’s submission, provide extended responses in the available spaces. For additional information, refer to the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs. For additional support with ML/ELL policy, please reach out to your respective ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance Performance Specialist; for support with ML/ELL instruction, please contact your ML/ELL Services Administrator.
