DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 196 of 304 Describe your English as a New Language (ENL), and if applicable Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE), and Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE) programs. For each program, include: How stand-alone ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How integrated ENL is implemented as per CR Part 154 How students are grouped (e.g., block [class travels together as a group], ungraded [all students regardless of grade are in one class], heterogeneous [mixed proficiency levels], homogenous [proficiency level is the same in one class]). a. English as a New Language (ENL) Stand-alone ENL: Students receiving instruction in ENL classrooms receive ENL support all day from their classroom teacher. Classes are formed with students with different language proficiency levels and teachers provide differentiated instruction to support the language and academic development for all students. One of our approaches to support language development is by following the SIOP Model, additionally all our services follow the push in model, in the stand- alone ENL classroom, the classroom teacher is the ENL teacher and delivers language supportive instruction to the students. Support is provided to students without the loss of classroom instruction or engaging learning time. The reading and writing progressions of the curriculum along with the New Language Arts Progressions are used to provide appropriate academic and developmental support to the students. Edwards Orton Gilingham’s structured, explicit, and multisensory approach helps students at different proficiency levels break down the complex code of English. It provides clear, systematic instruction in phonology, orthography, and syntax—all of which are critical for ELLs learning a new language. There are scheduled at least 360 minutes of ENL instruction a week in all of our ENL classroom environments. Integrated ENL: Instruction in this program, formerly known as English as a Second Language (ESL), emphasizes English language acquisition. In an ENL program, language arts and content-area instruction are taught in English using specific ENL instructional strategies. In an English as a New Language program students come from many different language backgrounds and are taught together. Students receiving instruction in ENL classrooms receive ENL support all day from their ENL teacher. Classes are formed with students with different language proficiency levels and teachers provide differentiated instruction to support the language and academic development for all students. We provide ENL support following the SIOP Model, all our services follow the push in model, where an ENL teacher delivers language supportive instruction to the student in the classroom while the student receives instruction from the general education or special education teacher. This is a collaborative way of providing language support to students without the loss of classroom instructional or engaging learning time. The general education teacher or special education teacher along with the Push-In/Pull-Out ENL teacher collaborate on curriculum and data analysis to target the needs of the students. The reading and writing progressions of the curriculum along with the New Language Arts Progressions are used to provide appropriate academic and developmental support to the students. The Edwards Orton-Gillingham (OG) approach in an Integrated ENL (English as a New Language) classroom is adapted to support both language and literacy development. The structured, explicit, and multisensory approach helps students at different proficiency levels break down the complex code of English. It provides clear, systematic instruction in phonology, orthography, and syntax—all of which are critical for ELLs learning a new language. There is at least 150 minutes of ENL/ELA instruction a day in all ENL classrooms. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 197 of 304 b. Bilingual Education (If applicable) Transitional Bilingual Education (TBE): N/A Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE): In order to meet the linguistic needs of our ELLs, parental choice and part 154 mandates, PS 94 Language Allocation Policy will be as follows: Dual Language 50% English 50% Spanish or Chinese, ENL Program Self Contained 100% English. All programs provide or go above the Part 154 mandates of providing 360 minutes a week of ENL for Entering and Emerging students; 180 to our Transitional and Expanding students and 90 minutes a week to our Commanding students for two additional years as per the new CR Part 154. All ELL students will receive 180 minutes a week of Home Language Arts. We use the NYSESLAT scores to update the proficiency level of each child to ensure that they are receiving the mandated minutes. Since all ELLs are in Dual Language Programs or self- contained ENL, the programming is set by student needs and teachers are able to adjust instruction based on proficiency levels of students. In addition, all instruction regardless of the content has a language objective. ENL instruction is integrated in all content areas including ELA, teachers also integrate their ENL instruction during the minilesson, as well as when teaching in small groups in their classrooms. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 198 of 304 2. How does your school ensure the mandated number of instructional minutes are provided according to students’ English language proficiency levels in each program model? One of our goals at PS 94 is to afford students the opportunity for high academic achievement while becoming bilingual, biliterate, and multicultural. We believe that these linguistic and cultural skills will enable them to function more successfully in a global society. We are strongly committed to providing our ELLs with the skills they need to be academically successful. Our guiding mission is to increase the academic achievement of our English Language Learners. To meet our mission we have heterogeneously grouped classes in Dual Language Spanish and Chinese in grades Pre K-5 as well as self contained ENL and integrated ICT classrooms in grades K-5. Every decision we make about the direction of our instruction is informed by the assessment of what our diverse student population does well and what it needs to improve. Our goals and objectives are designed to build upon the previous years of teaching and learning so we can focus on short term planning and long term planning for growth and improvement every year. We continuously compare our practice against best practices for gaps that give direction to our teaching and learning. Our weekly schedule and our flow of the day reflect the mandated requirements from the CR Part 154 in terms of ENL (standalone and integrated) and HLA. ENL Classes Grades K-5: Minutes Flow of the Day 20 minutes: Phonics in ENL 45 minutes: Reading in ENL 45 minutes: Writing and Grammar in ENL 45 minutes: Prep-Multicultural Focus 50 minutes: Lunch 45 minutes: Mathematics in ENL 45 minutes: Social Studies or Science in ENL Dual Language Self Contain Classes Dual Language Programs For the school year 2025-2026, we will continue our dual language programs as a one-way enrichment dual language programs, meaning enrollments of both Hispanic-American and Chinese- American heritages with a mix of English and Spanish or Chinese proficiency is present among the student population. The idea of an “enrichment- based program” is to bring these students together to teach each other the curriculum through their two heritage languages. (Collier, V. P., & Thomas, W. P. (2004).) In addition, the use of an enrichment based program is to create cognitive challenges through thematic units, with no translation and no repeated lessons in the other language, but instead building upon the understanding of skills and foundations of their L1 language to help their L2 language. Studies have shown that these types of programs that focus on real world problem solving stimulate students to make more than one year’s progress every year in both languages.(Collier, V. P., & Thomas, W. P. (2004). The goal of our programs align directly to district 15’s goal of having students learn how to speak, read, understand, and write in two languages, and also learn about and appreciate others’ cultures. (NYC Department of Education - District 15 Superintendent, 2022) For our K-5 classes we will be following a week by week language instruction schedule meaning; Week 1’s content instruction will be in English and week 2’s content instruction will be in home language with the exception of Social Emotional Learning, Heggerty, Phonological & Phonemic awareness, Vocabulary center in Grades 3-5 , and part of the writing component in the literacy blocks(K-2) and the full writing block in grades 3-5 . These subjects will always be taught in English in both weeks. How are ENL, English Language Arts (ELA) and if applicable, Home Language Arts (HLA) instructional minutes delivered in each program model? -To support the equitable and inclusive instruction of MLLs and ELLs, Tabletop Minilessons support language development and language acquisition and use in listening, speaking, reading, writing, and collaborative problem- solving. Students are provided with multiple embedded supports to meet the needs of all learners, including multiple access points, as well as scaffolded differentiation strategies and learning throughout the Modules in the Reading and Writing curriculum. -Students are provided with carefully selected, content-rich text sets to help students build topic knowledge and reading skills, digital texts, videos, graphic organizers, picture supports, language stems, sentence frames, multilingual glossary, and lists of cognates. Scaffold talking and writing about a text by providing word banks, sentence stems, and sentence frames. Students are provided repeated opportunities to develop strategies for learning academic vocabulary through the use of instructional strategies and supports to help students establish connections and develop their knowledge of content specific vocabulary. Hallmark 4 of Advanced Literacies (https://www.nysed.gov/sites/default/files/nov-8-nys_brief-6-of-8_-summer-2017_-hallmark-4-vocab_final_2.pdf- a.pdf) research indicates that ELA instruction provides students repeated opportunities to develop strategies for learning academic vocabulary through the use of research based word-learning strategies (eg. think alouds) and supports to help students establish connections and develop their bank of content specific vocabulary. -“El Dictado”meaning “The dictation” is an intentionally bilingual methodology to meet the linguistic and pedagogical needs of PS 94 students. It will be incorporated in English and the Home Language to teach and refine language arts skills, and to teach content, mechanics, including spelling, punctuation, and fluendy in a more integrated way. More DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 201 of 304 importantly, El Dictado allows students to develop metalinguistic awareness and cross language transference. -To ensure ELLs—have access to explicit, systematic instruction in phonics, phonemic awareness, and decoding, essential for reading success, Edwards Orton Gilingham’s structured, explicit, and multisensory approach helps students at different proficiency levels break down the complex code of English. It provides clear, systematic instruction in phonology, orthography, and syntax—all of which are critical for ELLs learning a new language. It helps students transfer literacy skills across languages, reinforcing foundational literacy skills alongside English acquisition. N/A DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 207 of 304 Part IV: Data Analysis Part IV: Data Analysis Data Analysis After reviewing and analyzing school demographic and assessment data, answer the following questions. The ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT) provided by the Office of Multilingual Learners can assist with identifying these data points. For additional information, refer to Data Analysis Professional Learning Module and ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT) Professional Learning Module. 1. What is the composition of the ELL subgroups at your school? What percentage of ELLs are Newcomers? How does effective instruction for Newcomers differ from those provided to Developing or Long-Term ELLs? At PS 94, 85.3% of ELLs are Newcomers. We have 1 student at PS 94 with inconsistent/ interrupted formal education (SIFE). -Newcomers students receive instructional support in the program they are enrolled : ENL or dual language. Newcomers will benefit from OG’s explicit and multisensory approach to learn the alphabet, sounds, and early decoding. We will use OG for basic sound-symbol relationships (alphabet, phonemic awareness), iIncorporating visuals, gestures, and realia with multisensory OG activities, including oral repetition and kinesthetic tracing to connect sounds to letters. -Developing ELLs are learning to build fluency and early academic skills. We will implement OG to teach blending, segmenting, and decoding skills, pairing OG word work with vocabulary visuals and sentence frames, using structured routines and small-group instruction for guided practice -Additionally, they are invited to participate in our English language arts after school program designed to develop vocabulary and address early reading strategies. They are also invited to participate in our weekend program which focuses on developing scaffolding strategies and vocabulary focus on phonics. Additionally, we also invite them to participate in our immigrant-newcomers theater after school program where they can explore the arts as they develop their understanding of the English language. Services for Long-Term ELLs-We currently do not have any long term ELL students. -Developing ELLs receive instructional support in the program they are enrolled in: ENL or dual language. -Developing ELLs will receive literacy instruction using the Edwards Orton-Gillingham approach, which offers a structured, multisensory, and explicit method for teaching foundational reading and writing skills. This approach is especially beneficial for newcomers as they begin to acquire English literacy. -Developing ELLs will benefit from using Rosetta Stone as a foundational resource for acquiring essential English language skills that focuses on building vocabulary, pronunciation, and basic grammar. By reinforcing listening, speaking, and visual recognition of words, it helps students gain confidence in their ability to understand and communicate in English. Rosetta Stone’s interactive approach provides the consistent, accessible practice that is crucial for early language learners as they build their foundational language skills. -LTELLs benefit more from instruction in Tier 2 and Tier 3 ENL support by one of the ENL service providers. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 208 of 304 What percentage of ELLs are Students with Inconsistent/Interrupted Formal Education (SIFE)? How do you differentiate effective instruction for SIFE? We have 1 SIFE student at PS 94. To support the equitable and inclusive instruction of our SIFE student, we use tabletop Minilessons to support language development and language acquisition and use in listening, speaking, reading, writing, and collaborative problem-solving. They are provided with multiple embedded supports to meet their learning needs, including multiple access points, as well as scaffolded differentiation strategies and learning throughout the Modules in the Reading and Writing curriculum. They are provided with carefully selected, content- rich text sets to help them build topic knowledge and reading skills, digital texts, videos, graphic organizers, picture supports, language stems, sentence frames, multilingual glossary, and lists of cognates. Scaffold talking and writing about a text by providing word banks, sentence stems, and sentence frames. They are provided word relationship activities, repeated exposure to content specific vocabulary through texts, videos, and experiential learning. The student is also provided repeated opportunities to develop strategies for learning academic vocabulary through the use of instructional strategies and supports to help them establish connections and develop their knowledge of content specific vocabulary. Integrating the Edwards Orton-Gillingham approach helps SIFE students build foundational literacy skills, especially when combined with strategies that support language development and background gaps. Rosetta Stone is used as a supplemental tool for supporting SIFE students to build oral language, listening comprehension, and everyday vocabulary. It plays a valuable role in language acquisition and confidence- building for newcomers with limited or interrupted schooling. In Mathematics, we provide ample and intentional opportunities for the student to engage in a balanced way with all four aspects of language—reading, writing, speaking, and listening while engaging with mathematics. Modeling teacher–student discourse and by providing suggestions for supported student-to-student discourse, educators provide opportunities for language-rich classrooms. High impact academic vocabulary is explicitly introduced and used repeatedly in various contexts to build familiarity and fluency. Teacher-led skill-based small group instruction is a part of every subject in our school where students receive differentiated instruction, equitable learning strategies, and teachers monitor skill learning during instruction. What percentage of ELLs are Long-Term ELLs? How does effective instruction for Long-Term ELLs differ from instruction designed for Developing ELLs? PS 94 does not have any Long-Term ELL students. At PS 94, 14.7% of ELLs are Developing ELLs students (4-6 years of services). -Developing ELLs receive instructional support in the program they are enrolled in: ENL or dual language. -Developing ELLs will receive literacy instruction using the Edwards Orton-Gillingham approach, which offers a structured, multisensory, and explicit method for teaching foundational reading and writing skills. This approach is especially beneficial for newcomers as they begin to acquire English literacy. -Developing ELLs will benefit from using Rosetta Stone as a foundational resource for acquiring essential English language skills that focuses on building vocabulary, pronunciation, and basic grammar. By reinforcing listening, speaking, and visual recognition of words, it helps students gain confidence in their ability to understand and communicate in English. Rosetta Stone’s interactive approach provides the consistent, accessible practice that is crucial for early language learners as they build their foundational language skills. -LTELLs benefit more from instruction in Tier 2 and Tier 3 ENL support by one of the ENL service providers. -LTELLS are encouraged to participate in the academic after school programs which focus on all four modalities of language. They are also invited to participate in the Saturday Academic Program which focuses on close reading strategies in both ELA and Mathematics for grades 3-5 or in our ENL Saturday program for grades 2 and 3. -LTELLs benefit from Rosetta Stone for supplemental practice, especially in oral fluency or pronunciation, refining language accuracy and filling academic language gaps. When you look at the results of the New York State English as a Second Language Achievement Test (NYSESLAT), what stands out? What trends are there for each modality? Overall, the trends suggest that while listening and speaking are strengths for many students, reading and writing need targeted interventions to support students’ English language development comprehensively. - Reading: The results indicate that reading is a modality where some students excel, yet there is also a significant need for improvement. In the Spring 2024 NYSESLAT, multiple grade levels showed a mix of high and low scores in reading. For example, students in grade 1 had 40 students scoring high, while the same grade had a number of students scoring low in this modality. - Writing: Writing appears to be a challenging area for many students. The data from Spring 2024 shows that while some grades, like grade 1, had students scoring high (31 students), there were also considerable low scores across various grades. Grade 5 has no students scoring high in the Spring 2024 NYSESLAT. This suggests that writing remains a critical area for development, especially for upper grades. - Listening: Listening is a relative strength for the students. A high number of students in grade 1, 2 and 3 performed well in this modality during the Spring 2024 NYSESLAT, indicating good comprehension skills when listening. This modality consistently shows fewer low scores compared to reading and writing. - Speaking: Speaking is another area where students tend to perform better. For example, grade 3 had 40 students scoring high in the Spring 2024 NYSESLAT. However, there are still students who struggle with speaking, as evidenced by lower scores in the 4th grade. 8. What is the relationship between students’ performance on the NYSESLAT and other state assessments such as the ELA assessment, the Math assessment, and the Regents exams? Students who perform well on the NYSESLAT tend to also achieve higher scores on the ELA and Math assessments. This shows that proficiency in English language skills, as measured by the NYSESLAT, is closely related to academic performance in other subjects. For example, students who scored at “Commanding” level in NYSESLAT generally also scored at 3 or 4 on ELA and Math. Students who scored at “Entering” or “Emerging” typically scored 1 or 2 on the ELA and Math. This indicates that limited English proficiency can hinder students' ability to perform well in other state assessments that require strong language skills. Students who show annual progress on the NYSESLAT also tend to improve their scores on the ELA and Math assessments. This positive correlation highlights the importance of continuous language development in enhancing overall academic performance. For instance, students moving from the "Emerging" to "Transitioning" levels on the NYSESLAT often see corresponding improvements in their ELA and Math scores. Some students may perform well in one or two modalities of the NYSESLAT in listening and speaking, but continue to struggle with academic language required for the ELA and Math assessment. 9. Tier 1 Core Instruction 1. How does your school provide ELLs with a high-quality academic experience that leads to deeper learning? By integrating language development into core instruction, differentiating based on proficiency, and using inclusive practices, our Tier 1 instruction equips ELLs with the tools they need for deep learning, academic success, and confident participation in all classroom activities. Key Elements of High-Quality Tier 1 Instruction for ELLs include: -Integrated Language and Content Instruction to make grade-level content comprehensible while promoting English language development. Language objectives are embedded in every lesson, alongside content objectives. - Scaffolds and supports including Instruction with visual aids, realia, sentence frames, graphic organizers, anchor charts, and modeling. Teachers provide comprehensible input through repetition, rephrasing, and simplified explanations without diluting content. - Collaborative Learning and Structured Talk with ELLs participating in collaborative conversations, group work, and structured academic discourse routines (e.g., Think-Pair-Share, Accountable Talk). Oral language is developed through meaningful peer interactions using sentence stems and discussion prompts. -Differentiated Instruction is adjusted based on language proficiency levels. ELLs receive targeted small-group support during Tier 1 instructional blocks (e.g., shared reading/interactive read aloud, vocabulary stations). -Culturally Responsive Practices include teachers integrating students’ backgrounds, experiences, and home languages into instruction. Texts and materials reflect cultural diversity, fostering engagement and belonging. Lessons are language-rich and include explicit vocabulary instruction to help ELLs connect sounds and meaning in English. - Ongoing Formative Assessment in which teachers use language assessments to check understanding and adjust instruction in real-time. ELLs are assessed using multiple modalities (listening, speaking, reading, writing) to show what they know. - Literacy with Edwards Orton-Gillingham (OG) approach during Tier 1 phonics instruction provides a structured, multisensory way to build phonemic awareness, decoding, and encoding—critical for developing English literacy. 2. How does your school’s leadership team ensure ELLs have access to core instruction/curriculum? The Instructional Leadership Team of PS 94 spends considerable time and energy focusing on the needs of ML’s and ELL’s when designing our school’s instructional focus. ML’s, ELL’s, and Former ELL’s are our largest subgroup of DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 216 of 304 students; 51.7% of our students are ELL’s and 19.1% are former ELL’s. Since more than three quarters of our student population are in need of specialized and targeted English language instruction, our ILT considers the community’s diverse linguistic and identity needs in every aspect of our curriculum, programming, and instruction. The first and most important step is our staff development. Not only do the teachers and support staff of the school reflect the cultural and linguistic identities of the student population, as at least half of our staff are multilingual, but we also spend considerable time during staff development discussing the importance of getting to know one’s students beyond their academic needs. By developing the mindset and understanding that ELL’s are not deficient in English, but proficient in another language and culture amongst our staff, we are setting a goal and a standard that our ELL’s will achieve from the top down. Next, the ILT has discussed and enacted several changes to our programming to better support teachers and clusters in delivering instruction that can be modified per student need; the creation of out of classroom STEM and Social Studies teachers that can deliver content in English, Spanish and Mandarin allow classroom teachers to focus on ELA and Math. This process is ongoing and will allow all teachers more time to work collaboratively on how to best modify and align instruction for each and every student, including ML’s, ELL’s, students with IEP’s and any other subgroup. Finally, the ILT has had many intense discussions and reflections on curriculum modification. As we’ve deepened our understanding of our students and their diverse backgrounds, we’ve taken intentional steps to make our classroom libraries more culturally responsive. We have curated a broader range of texts that reflect the languages, cultures, histories, and perspectives of our multilingual learners. Our collections now include own-voice authors, stories featuring diverse protagonists, and books that affirm students' identities while also building empathy and global awareness. In addition, we are using these texts as mentor texts during instruction to: validate students' backgrounds and experiences, foster inclusive discussions, promote social- emotional learning through relatable narratives, and support language development by providing rich, meaningful contexts for vocabulary and comprehension. We have been engaging in curriculum modification as far as best instructional practices for quite some time; our current focus is how we can align the curriculum so that our ML’s and ELL’s see their languages, their cultures, their families and themselves in the books that they read, the problems they solve, and the projects they create. By holistically developing our staff, program, and curriculum to the linguistic and identity needs of our student population, we are creating a learning environment that is responsive to and considerate of their needs and intellectual development. 3. Describe how core content is delivered in each program model. Specify language, and the instructional approaches and methods to foster language development and meet the demands of the Next Generation Learning Standards. Mathematics All classes from Kindergarten through Fifth Grade will deliver Great Minds- Eureka Math Squared program as the core instructional program as well as Math Exemplars and i-Ready as supplemental resources. These programs combine to create a comprehensive mathematics program that is aligned to the Next Generation Learning Standards, integrates and solidifies a progression of skills with conceptual learning practices to help all students master 21st Century math skills. In addition, through the Mathematical Teaching Practices, students develop a healthy mathematics identity in a socially, emotionally, and academically safe environment for mathematics teaching and learning. PS94 Mathematics Expectations are aligned to the National Council of Teachers of Mathematics Recognize that each and every student, from Pre-Kindergarten through Fifth grade, is able to solve challenging mathematical tasks successfully. Build in each student positive mathematical identity and a sense of agency Design instruction that builds on students prior knowledge and experiences Teach in ways that ensure that each and every student is reasoning and making sense of mathematics on a daily basis Reflect on ways that tasks and teaching can be improved to provide greater access, challenge and support for every learner Great Minds- Eureka Math Squared: This program offers teachers the opportunity to differentiate learning experiences for all students. By teaching mathematics as a “story”, Eureka Math builds students’ knowledge logically and thoroughly to help them achieve a deep understanding. This program incorporates a balanced approach of fluency activities, concept development, and applications, to support the development of an increasingly complex understanding of mathematical skills. It is not enough for students to know the process for solving a problem; they need to understand why that process works. Student problem solving experiences are a progression of the pedagogical approach of the DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 217 of 304 concrete, pictorial, and abstract stages of learning in combination with writing with reasoning to express their mathematical thinking. The instructional design of Eureka Math Squared develops engaging learning experiences of our students that are more inclusive, culturally responsive and supportive of special populations (SWDs, and MLLs). As students engage in the learning, lessons and modules increase exponentially in mathematical rigor to help students build enduring math knowledge. More specifically, Eureka Math Squared provides SWOA’s and MLLs with increased opportunities of intentional student discourse and a deliberate progression of engagement. The Eureka Math Squared program will supports teachers to deliver and students to learn the following: - establish a foundational understanding of mathematics (the why) rather than only relying on procedural skills (the how) - strengthen reasoning and critical-thinking skills through student discourse -develop a productive struggle by addressing the variability of all learners through the Universal Design for Learning principles that are explicitly provided in all lessons. For the 2025-2026 school year, we will continue to collaborate with Social Studies and STEM departments. Through this collaboration, we will foster an interdisciplinary approach that focuses on PBLs. As students engage in authentic projects of their choice, they will be provided opportunities to extend topic knowledge by engaging in deeper research and developing authentic project showcases. In addition to students developing content reading, writing, listening and speaking skills, they will also understand and integrate the authenticity of real-life mathematical connections and skills that align to the authentic projects of their choice. Great Minds- Eureka Math Squared Supporting Multi-lingual Learners and Students with other Abilities (SWDs)- Eureka Math Squared provides multiple supports to meet the academic, language and learning style variability of all learners. The instructional design, instructional routines, and lesson-specific strategies support teachers as they address learner variance and support students with understanding, speaking, and writing English in mathematical contexts. The language support embedded in all lessons, across all grades, provides ideas to support students with receiving (reading and listening) and producing (speaking and writing) English in mathematical contexts. Suggestions may include ways to promote student-to-student discourse, support new and familiar content-specific terminology or academic language, or support students with multiple meaning words. The Eureka Math Squared program promotes student discourse by employing routines that encourage student-to-student dialogue, using open-ended questions and scenarios to generate opportunities for authentic class discussion, and suggesting when students might use the leveled sentence starters provided in the Talking Tool to participate in discussions. The instructional guidance that appears in the lesson’s blue margin notes provides information about facilitation, differentiation, and coherence and supports teachers to plan for learner variability for SWOAs and MLLs. Within each lesson, across all grade levels, there are six types of instructional guidance: teacher notes (inform pedagogical choices), Universal Design for Learning (UDL), language support, differentiation to challenge and support, mathematical practices, math past. The Universal Design for Learning (UDL) framework guides the construction of the entire curriculum, informing its use of math models, simple-to-complex progressions, routines, and discourse so that all students can understand the why behind the math and productively engage in math learning. Ultimately, the Eureka Math Squared program prioritizes student engagement by maximizing the number of students actively participating at any given time. Finally, All Eureka Math Squared materials are translated in both English and Spanish. Our Dual language Chinese teachers translate students' resources, assessments and instructional materials to teach our students in the dual language Chinese program. Science, Technology, Engineering, Mathematics (STEM) Our STEM team uses Amplify Science, for grades K-5. Amplify Science aligns to the Next Generation Learning Standards by blending hands-on investigations, literacy-rich activities, and interactive digital tools to empower students to think, read, write, and argue like real scientists and engineers. Each unit of Amplify Science engages students in a relevant, real-world problem where they investigate scientific phenomena, engage in collaboration and discussion, and develop models or explanations in order to arrive at solutions. Students are: introduced to a phenomenon and a related problem, provided opportunities to collect evidence from multiple sources (digital, text, simulations, hands-on, and observations), build increasingly complex explanations, and apply knowledge to solve a different problem. Students are taught through a multimodal approach, which allows students to experience learning in a variety of ways to create diverse learning styles. All Amplify units, chapters, and lessons are grounded in the Do, Talk, Read, Write, Visualize approach. PBL is integrated into each unit. Students are exposed to a real world phenomena and investigate this phenomena throughout the unit using hands-on activities, multimedia resources, research, field trips, discourse, and writing to apply their learning and solve a similar real world problem. Students show their learning by creating models, visuals, and writing pieces. After completing a project, students present their projects to other students and/or parents and peer assess and self-assess their work. Students not only understand content DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 218 of 304 more deeply but also learn how to take responsibility, build confidence, solve problems, work collaboratively, communicate ideas, and be creative innovators. This year, we will continue to integrate an interdisciplinary approach with PBLs through Science by engaging children in projects that provide opportunities to extend topic knowledge while building research, writing, listening, speaking, and collaboration skills in ELA. Using inquiry and research through an interdisciplinary curriculum, learners will use their thinking skills to formulate questions about the world, find appropriate and accurate information, and make thoughtful judgments about their findings. In each module, students complete a focused, inquiry-based project paced over several weeks. Children research, collaborate, and complete a project about the topic of the module. Through inquiry and research projects, children can practice these critical skills and learn that the process of finding answers to their questions can be joyful, reflective and inquisitive. Project based learning aligns with and allows for students to strengthen their Science and Engineering practices. Amplify Science allows project based learning to be taught through a 3 dimensional approach, with a broad range of experiences. 3-D learning engages students, allows them to build their science and engineering practices, and apply cross-cutting concepts to develop understanding and solve challenging problems connected to disciplinary core ideas. Students will be: Asking questions (for science) and defining problems (for engineering) Developing and using models Planning and carrying out investigations Analyzing and interpreting data Using mathematics and computational thinking Constructing explanations (for science) and designing solutions (for engineering) Engaging in arguments from evidence Obtaining, evaluating, and communicating information Amplify Science is designed with diversity and equity in mind. They believe “diversity in the science and engineering classroom is an asset. It offers countless opportunities for creativity and innovation and opens the door to multiple perspectives and cross-cultural understanding.” With this belief in mind they have designed the curriculum to provide quality and supportive materials for teachers so that every student regardless of their background, where they live, the language they speak at home, or their learning characteristics — has access to and benefits from deep and engaging science and engineering learning opportunities. Two overarching conceptual frameworks informed Amplify Science’s approach to ensuring access and equity for all students: Universal Design for Learning and Culturally and Linguistically Responsive Teaching. This year our STEM program has been integrated with Social Emotional Learning and a focus on the CASEL competencies. Students will be provided opportunities to strengthen their self-awareness, self- management, social awareness, relationship skills, and responsible decision making. All units have integrated the HEART Curriculum, everyday circle cards, Humane Education Resource Guide, Kindness for all guide, and the Mood Meter. Students will have multiple times throughout a unit to revisit and enhance social emotional skills. Unit PBL/ projects have been modified to allow for students to use their social emotional learning when solving real world problems particularly in the areas of social awareness, relationship skills, and responsible decision making. Amplify Science aligns to Hallmark 4 of Advance Literacy in studying a small set of high utility words. Amplify Science understands that language development involves understanding the meanings of words and phrases heard or read, as well as using those words and phrases to communicate effectively in speech or writing. Students are provided repeated opportunities to develop strategies for learning academic vocabulary through the use of instructional strategies and supports to help students establish connections and develop their knowledge of content specific language. Students are provided word relationship activities, repeated exposure to content specific vocabulary through texts, videos, and hands on activities. Amplify Science provides multiple supports to meet the needs of all learners, and includes multiple access points for students throughout the unit. Students are provided digital texts, print materials, videos, picture supports, language stems/frames, and multilingual glossary. Each unit of Amplify science has a differentiation section in the lesson brief for each lesson. This section provides embedded supports for diverse learners and tools for differentiation, as well as specific differentiation strategies for English Learners. These sections are tailored to each lesson. Students are provided a multimodal approach. A Multimodal learning approach teaches concepts using multiple modes to provide information to students including but not limited to the sue of pictures, texts, modeling, hands on activities, and videos. Monitoring Student Learning All students will engage in formal and informal assessment opportunities to demonstrate understanding and for teachers to gather information throughout the unit. Students have the opportunity to take a Pre-Unit / End of unit Assessment for every unit. The pre-unit assessment is an opportunity for students to articulate their initial ideas and understanding about the unit topic. It allows for teachers to see what concepts students already understand and what misconceptions the students have. The end of unit assessment will be graded with the end of unit rubric. The DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 219 of 304 end of unit assessment written explanations show each student’s level of understanding of the core concepts from the unit, as well as provide evidence of growth over time when compared with their pre-unit assessments. In each grade, there are multiple opportunities to monitor student progress through the use of the Critical Juncture assessments and On the Fly assessments. Critical Juncture assessments signify points in the unit at which it is especially important that all students understand the content before continuing. They are designed to provide formative information about the actual level at which each student understands the content by the end of each of the lessons indicated. Each Critical Juncture Assessment includes specific suggestions for how to tailor instructional opportunities so they are targeted to a student’s current level of understanding. On the Fly assessments are quick checks for understanding designed to help the teacher monitor and support students’ progress throughout the unit. On-the-Fly Assessments are provided for nearly every lesson. Each On-the-Fly Assessment indicates the specific concepts and practices to look for (or listen for) as students engage with the learning experiences, followed by suggestions to the teacher of what to do, based on what was observed. These formative assessments are embedded in the instruction and do not require formal scoring of students’ work. Students should have a STEM folder that highlights their work. In their folders students should have the opportunity to include: Samples of work from key learning areas, Writing samples, Investigation pages, Student self-reflections, Photographs of work, Pre and End of unit assessments, and Final Projects. We will continue to use the Passports to Social Studies program in kindergarten through fifth grade. Our Social Studies curriculum has been enhanced in order to not only include the Passports to Social Studies curriculum, Civics for All, and Social-Emotional CASEL Competencies curriculum, but we have also integrated the equity framework from Cultivating Genius named the Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework by Dr. Gholdy Muhammad, which addresses students’ Identities, Skills, Intellect, Criticality, and Joy. The Passports to Social Studies program aligns to the NYC Social Studies Scope & Sequence and integrates the New York State Social Studies Framework to support effective and engaging social studies teaching and learning. Most importantly, students use rich content, themes, and big ideas to learn history, geography, economics, civics, citizenship, and government. They also use important skills to “think like historians.” The Passports to Social Studies program units of study are guided by an essential question. The essential question is multi-faceted and is designed for open discussion and interpretation and can be applied beyond the content of one specific unit. Each lesson from the Passports to Social Studies program and Civics for All is guided by a focus question to direct students on developing the relevant skills, practices, and knowledge objectives that lead to historical understanding. Students are challenged to think like historians and encouraged to raise questions, think critically, consider many perspectives, and gather evidence in support of their interpretations through the practice of chronological processing, decision-making, and historical research and analysis. These real-world skills will serve students well as participating citizens of a democracy and as productive adults in the future. Passports are translated to Chinese and Spanish, and we follow the 50 – 50 model for our dual-language programs. P.S. 94 is a Civics for All partner school. Civics for All is a companion to the Passport to Social Studies, designed with the New York State K-12 Social Studies Framework, the New York State Next Generation English Language Arts & Literacy in History/Social Studies Learning Standards, and the New York City K-12 Social Studies Scope & Sequence in mind. Our teachers translate students' resources and instructional materials to Spanish and Chinese to teach our students in the dual-language programs. The Civics for All initiative strengthens opportunities for students to develop skills and knowledge related to democracy. The goal is for all our students to become engaged citizens who can think critically and are empowered to chart the course that our city and country will take. Civics for All helps schools support students in making connections between history, current events, and democratic structures. Civics for All lessons are incorporated into Passports to Social Studies units and lessons to cultivate civic responsibility and awareness in our students to become active and informed participants of our democratic society. The Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework from Cultivating Genius by Dr. Gholdy Muhammad is a universal teaching and learning model that helps teachers cultivate the genius within students and within themselves and teach in ways that create space for mutual empowerment, confidence, and self-reliance. The Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework is an equity framework that believes every time children of color come together to read, write, think, and learn, they are making sense of who they are through Identity, developing their proficiencies in the content they are learning within through Skills, becoming smarter about something or gaining new knowledge and concepts in the world through Intellect, and developing the ability to read texts to understand power, authority and anti-oppression through Criticality. These five goals or pursuits are connected to the body of literacy research on cognitive and sociocultural perspectives of literacy development and what youth need to attain personal and academic success: Identity: It is composed of who DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 220 of 304 we are, who others say we are, and who we desire to be. Youth need opportunities in school to explore multiple facets of selfhood, but also learn about the identities of others who may differ. Knowing about the cultures of other people teaches them how to respect, love, and live in harmony with others who don’t look or know the world as they do. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction help students to learn something about themselves and/or about others?” Skills: It is essential for today’s youth to read, write, and think about diverse sets of literature in multimodal forms in order to develop their literacy skills. It is important that students develop the skills necessary in not only literacy but in all content areas. In particular, the skill of writing development should be cultivated in each content area because it entails the act of reading something, thinking about the content, considering new ideas to set on paper, and then communicating those words to an intended audience. Skills give across areas of learning students access and tools for education. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction build students’ skills for the content area?” Intellect: Intellect or knowledge is what we learn or understand about various topics, concepts, and paradigms. Through the pursuit of Identity and Skills, the possibility is created for Intellect, and when students develop intellectualism, they can express their ideas, work through justice-centered solutions to the world’s problems and expand their mental capacities. Intellect is not only about academics, but also emotional intelligence and self-and social awareness. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction build students’ knowledge and mental powers?” Criticality: It is the capacity to read, write, and think in ways of understanding how power, privilege, social justice, equity, and oppression operate throughout society particularly for populations who have been historically marginalized in the world. It enables students to question both the world and texts within it to better understand the truth in history, power, and equity. Teaching criticality humanizes instruction and makes it more compassionate. It begins to give students the tools they need to respond to injustice in and around schools and to tell the difference between facts and truths. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction engage students’ thinking about power and equity and the disruption of oppression?” Joy: This is another aspect of the framework because it’s essential to maintain one’s journey. All the pursuits are collaborative and encourage others to participate. When we offer a variety of subjects to excite students’ interest, bring joy and pleasure from reading, and stimulate their minds to think and debate about significant issues, we create an environment that affords students the opportunity to shape their own ideas through acts of literacy. It is our job as educators to not just teach skills, but teach students to know, validate, & celebrate who they are. As an educator, ask yourself, “How am I elevating beauty in humanity?” By implementing Passports to Social Studies, Civics for All, SocialEmotional CASEL Competencies, and the Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework we will prepare our students to be productive citizens of a global community and to nurture and promote global awareness. For our students to be globally aware citizens, students must understand and be able to: connect the local and the global, understanding of how the actions of people around the planet have an economic, technological, and cultural influence on all peoples of the world. participate in local and global economies. be open-minded, especially in understanding one’s own cultural lens as well as others’ distinct cultural lenses. celebrate similarities among different groups of people. understand and respect peoples’ differences. use electronic technologies in order to research people and cultures in every world region. understand the importance of cross-cultural communication, both within the United States and across borders. recognize and reduce stereotypes and prejudices. have compassion for all peoples of the world. assume responsibility for the ways in which they process information and read the world with a critical eye, refusing to accept unexamined information as factual or truth. This school year Passports to Social Studies and Civics for All has been integrated with Social Emotional CASEL Competencies. As students engage in historical explorations, global perspectives, historical literacies, and cultural studies, they will be able to deepen their connections and strengthen their personal social emotional skills in the areas of self-awareness, self-management, social awareness, relationships skills, and responsible decision making. This year Social Studies units of study maps will continue to have an essential question along with a social emotional focus question aligned to the content as well as SEL competency. Students will have multiple opportunities throughout a unit to revisit and enhance social emotional skills using complimentary lessons from Civics for All, and HEART resources such as Everyday Circle Cards, Humane Education Resource Guide, Kindness for All Guide, and the R.U.L.E.R Mood Meter. Project-Based Learning PBL is an effective and enjoyable way to learn and develop deeper learning competencies required for success in college, career and civic life. Students are active, not passive; a project engages their hearts and minds, and provides real world relevance for learning. PBL engages students in experiential learning, they work on projects that are aligned to content grade expectations as they work together solving a problem that has relevance to the real world. After completing a project, students present their projects to other students and/or parents and peer assess and self- DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 221 of 304 assess their work. Students who gain content knowledge with PBL are better able to apply what they know and can do to new situations. Students not only understand content more deeply but also learn how to take responsibility and build confidence, solve problems, work collaboratively, communicate ideas, and be creative innovators. The Next Generation standards emphasize real-world application of knowledge and skills, and the development of 21st century competencies such as critical thinking, communication in a variety of media, and collaboration. PBL provides an effective way to address such standards. Modern technology – which students use so much in their lives – is a perfect fit with PBL. With technology, teachers and students can connect with experts, partners, and audiences around the world, and use tech tools to find resources and information, create products, and collaborate more effectively. PBL is integrated throughout the units of study and our learning maps. PBL is an effective and enjoyable way to learn and develop deeper learning competencies required for success in college, career and civic life. Students are active, not passive; a project engages their hearts and minds, and provides real-world relevance for learning. PBL engages students in experiential learning, they work on projects that are aligned to content grade expectations as they work together solving a problem that has relevance to the real world. During in-person, synchronous and asynchronous instruction students can demonstrate their learning by creating models, visuals, and writing pieces through student-led research projects intended to reinforce, extend or apply lesson objectives or independent student goals. After completing a project, students present their projects to other students and/or parents and peer assess and self-assess their work. Students not only understand content more deeply but also learn how to take responsibility and build confidence, solve problems, work collaboratively, communicate ideas, and be creative innovators. Project based learning aligns and allows students to strengthen their Social Studies and Civics practices. This year, we will continue to integrate an interdisciplinary approach with PBLs through Social Studies by engaging children in projects that provide opportunities to extend topic knowledge while building research, writing, listening, speaking, and collaboration skills in ELA. Using inquiry and research through an interdisciplinary curriculum, learners will use their thinking skills to formulate questions about the world, find appropriate and accurate information, and make thoughtful judgments about their findings. In each module, students complete a focused, inquiry-based project paced over several weeks. Children research, collaborate, and complete a project about the topic of the module. Through inquiry and research projects, children can practice these critical skills and learn that the process of finding answers to their questions can be joyful, reflective and inquisitive. We will continue to incorporate Mathematics into our PBLs. Math is easy to link to economics, but it can be connected to history, civics, and geography. The study of human social relationships is studied using mathematical models. Mathematical analysis is also used to analyze social networks, how humans are connected to each other, and how they behave in various social situations. It is important not to think of integration as using the particular subject to solve math problems, but as a way to use math to solve problems and answer questions about those topics in Social Studies. Both disciplines use words such as compare, explain, articulate, analyze, predict, demonstrate and interpret and should be used to make connections between both disciplines. Some ways we will integrate mathematics into our Social Studies PBLs is through map skills (latitude and longitude), shapes in our community, calculating distances (fractions), number sense (ability to analyze numbers), and different nationalities and their number systems (Egypt, China, etc.). Students who can make connections between Math and Social Studies are able to think critically and understand history, culture, and politics. Teachers will engage in ongoing collaboration to develop PBLs that are aligned to both Social Studies and Mathematics. Assessing Students and Monitoring Student Learning Assessments of students are essential in monitoring student learning and growth. Students will engage in formal and DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 222 of 304 informal assessment opportunities to demonstrate understanding and for teachers to gather assessment data throughout the unit. Informal assessment examples may include, but are not limited to teacher created checklists, student/teacher created questionnaires, writing samples, class discussions, and student portfolios. Students will take Pre-Unit Assessment and End-of-Unit Assessment for every unit. The pre-unit assessment will be administered prior to the start of a new unit. It is an opportunity for students to articulate their initial ideas and understanding about the unit topic. It allows for teachers to see what concepts students already understand and what misconceptions students may have. The end of unit assessment may consist of Performance Tasks, Formative Assessments, or Extension Activities as outlined in Passports to Social Studies Teachers Guide or Civics for All Teachers Guide. In a Performance Task Assessment at the end of units, students are assessed based on their abilities to: compare/contrast, illustrate similarities and differences, illustrate bias or point of view, describe change over time, discuss issues categorically: socially, economically, politically, explain causes and effects of historic events, and or examine contending perspectives on an issue. In a Formative Assessment, students are assessed based on their abilities to successfully complete a project illustrating understanding of the unit topic. Extension Activities assessments are units which include relevant Civics for All PBL projects. These end of unit assessments provide evidence of growth over time when compared with their pre-unit assessments data. All students will use iLearnNYC portfolios to monitor their growth. Digital portfolios will be used to document the learning process and/or growth of students, and for formative assessments. To manage or import evidence, students and or teachers can use the portfolio tool within iLearnNYC, or through the mobile App. to submit work digitally. Students or teachers may include the following into the paper and/or digital portfolios: Samples of work Writing samples Workbook pages Student self-reflection. Teachers use the HMH Into Reading curriculum to introduce vocabulary, reading strategies, and writing skills. Lessons support grade-level expectations by integrating literacy standards by identifying the skills in each module, while gradually building access through scaffolds. HMH’s emphasis on authentic texts, scaffolded questions, and writing frames helps ELLs meet both language and literacy goals. Lessons are scaffolded using visual supports, sentence frames, graphic organizers, and realia. Emphasis is placed on listening, speaking, reading, and writing in context, with guided practice and repetition. Oral language is built through structured discussion routines, choral reading, and interactive read-alouds. ELLs develop academic vocabulary and syntax through close reading, collaborative discussions, and writing tasks. Teachers may use side-by-side texts, glossaries, or home language mentors to bridge concepts across languages building biliteracy and metalinguistic awareness by comparing how language works across English and the home language. Home language support accelerates conceptual understanding, while English instruction gradually increases in complexity. Teachers adapt content using intentional language supports and differentiated strategies to ensure that English learners develop both the language proficiency and academic skills required to meet the Next Generation Learning Standards. To ensure ELLs have access to explicit, systematic instruction in phonics, phonemic awareness, and decoding, essential for reading success, Edwards Orton Gilingham’s structured, explicit, and multisensory approach helps students at different proficiency DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 223 of 304 levels break down the complex code of English. It provides clear, systematic instruction in phonology, orthography, and syntax—all of which are critical for ELLs learning a new language. It helps students transfer literacy skills across languages, reinforcing foundational literacy skills alongside English acquisition. Rosetta Stone is used as a supplemental language development tool to support English Language Learners (ELLs) in building essential communication skills. Its primary focus is on developing oral language fluency, listening comprehension, and everyday vocabulary through an immersive, interactive learning environment. Rosetta Stone immerses students in English through spoken language and visual context, helping them develop an ear for English pronunciation, rhythm, and intonation. Through speech recognition technology, students practice speaking and receive immediate feedback on pronunciation and fluency. Vocabulary is contextualized through visuals and scenarios, promoting deeper understanding and long-term retention. Students listen to native-speaker models and match spoken language to images, strengthening auditory processing and word recognition. 4. The following components will be used as Summative assessments: Acadience (K-2) Screener Beginning of Year Assessment Acadience (K-2) Screener Middle of Year Assessment Acadience (K-2) Screener End of Year Assessment MAP (3 -5) Screener Beginning of Year Assessment MAP (3 -5) Screener Middle of Year Assessment MAP (3 -5) Screener End of Year Assessment I-Ready Reading Diagnostic: (K-5) Diagnostic administration #1 I-Ready Reading Diagnostic: Diagnostic administration #2 I-Ready Reading Diagnostic: Diagnostic administration #3 The Edwards Orton-Gillingham (OG) approach will be used to support the development of foundational literacy skills for students who require explicit, multisensory instruction—particularly English Language Learners (ELLs), students with reading difficulties, and those identified for Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions. This structured, sequential, and diagnostic approach will help students build phonemic awareness, decoding skills, encoding (spelling), and reading fluency, while reinforcing language acquisition. By incorporating OG into small-group and targeted instruction, the school will ensure that all learners, especially those with diverse needs, are given equitable access to the tools they need to become successful, confident readers. In alignment with the New York State Next Generation Learning Standards, the OG approach will help ensure that all students are equipped with strong foundational reading skills, particularly in phonemic awareness, decoding, fluency, vocabulary, and spelling. Teachers will deliver OG-aligned lessons in small-group settings, where instruction can be closely matched to students’ specific literacy needs, as identified through formative assessments, i-Ready data, and MTSS referral processes. Science, Technology, Engineering, Mathematics (STEM) Our STEM team uses Amplify Science, for grades K-5. Amplify Science aligns to the Next Generation Learning Standards by blending hands-on investigations, literacy-rich activities, and interactive digital tools to empower students to think, read, write, and argue like real scientists and engineers. Each unit of Amplify Science engages students in a relevant, real-world problem where they investigate scientific phenomena, engage in collaboration and discussion, and develop models or explanations in order to arrive at solutions. Students are: introduced to a phenomenon and a related problem, provided opportunities to collect evidence from multiple sources (digital, text, simulations, hands-on, and observations), build increasingly complex explanations, and apply knowledge to solve a different problem. Students are taught through a multimodal approach, which allows students to experience learning in a variety of ways to create diverse learning styles. All Amplify units, chapters, and lessons are grounded in the Do, Talk, Read, Write, Visualize approach. PBL is integrated into each unit. Students are exposed to a real world phenomena and investigate this phenomena throughout the unit using hands- on activities, multimedia resources, research, field trips, discourse, and writing to apply their learning and solve a similar real world problem. Students show their learning by creating models, visuals, and writing pieces. After completing a project, students present their projects to other students and/or parents and peer assess and self- DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 225 of 304 assess their work. Students not only understand content more deeply but also learn how to take responsibility, build confidence, solve problems, work collaboratively, communicate ideas, and be creative innovators. Project based learning aligns with and allows for students to strengthen their Science and Engineering practices. Amplify Science allows project based learning to be taught through a 3 dimensional approach, with a broad range of experiences. 3- D learning engages students, allows them to build their science and engineering practices, and apply cross-cutting concepts to develop understanding and solve challenging problems connected to disciplinary core ideas. Students will be: Asking questions (for science) and defining problems (for engineering) Developing and using models Planning and carrying out investigations Analyzing and interpreting data Using mathematics and computational thinking Constructing explanations (for science) and designing solutions (for engineering) Engaging in arguments from evidence Obtaining, evaluating, and communicating information Amplify Science is designed with diversity and equity in mind. They believe “diversity in the science and engineering classroom is an asset. It offers countless opportunities for creativity and innovation and opens the door to multiple perspectives and cross-cultural understanding.” With this belief in mind they have designed the curriculum to provide quality and supportive materials for teachers so that every student regardless of their background, where they live, the language they speak at home, or their learning characteristics — has access to and benefits from deep and engaging science and engineering learning opportunities. Two overarching conceptual frameworks informed Amplify Science’s approach to ensuring access and equity for all students: Universal Design for Learning and Culturally and Linguistically Responsive Teaching. This year our STEM program has been integrated with Social Emotional Learning and a focus on the CASEL competencies. Students will be provided opportunities to strengthen their self-awareness, self- management, social awareness, relationship skills, and responsible decision making. All units have integrated the HEART Curriculum, everyday circle cards, Humane Education Resource Guide, Kindness for all guide, and the Mood Meter. Students will have multiple times throughout a unit to revisit and enhance social emotional skills. Unit PBL/ projects have been modified to allow for students to use their social emotional learning when solving real world problems particularly in the areas of social awareness, relationship skills, and responsible decision making.
