In each grade, there are multiple opportunities to monitor student progress through the use of the Critical Juncture assessments and On the Fly assessments. Critical Juncture assessments signify points in the unit at which it is especially important that all students understand the content before continuing. They are designed to provide DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 226 of 304 formative information about the actual level at which each student understands the content by the end of each of the lessons indicated. Each Critical Juncture Assessment includes specific suggestions for how to tailor instructional opportunities so they are targeted to a student’s current level of understanding. On the Fly assessments are quick checks for understanding designed to help the teacher monitor and support students’ progress throughout the unit. On-the-Fly Assessments are provided for nearly every lesson. Each On-the-Fly Assessment indicates the specific concepts and practices to look for (or listen for) as students engage with the learning experiences, followed by suggestions to the teacher of what to do, based on what was observed. These formative assessments are embedded in the instruction and do not require formal scoring of students’ work. Students should have a STEM folder that highlights their work. In their folders students should have the opportunity to include: Samples of work from key learning areas Writing samples Investigation pages Student self-reflections Photographs of work Pre and End of unit assessments Final Projects Social Studies How do we teach Social Studies at P.S. 94-Historically Responsive Literacy Framework For the 2025-2026 school year, as per 2025-2026 school year, we will continue to use the Passports to Social Studies program in kindergarten through fifth grade. Our Social Studies curriculum has been enhanced in order to not only include the Passports to Social Studies curriculum, Civics for All, and Social- Emotional CASEL Competencies curriculum, but we have also integrated the equity framework from Cultivating Genius named the Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework by Dr. Gholdy Muhammad, which addresses students’ Identities, Skills, Intellect, Criticality, and Joy. The Passports to Social Studies program aligns to the NYC Social Studies Scope & Sequence and integrates the New York State Social Studies Framework to support effective and engaging social studies teaching and learning. Most importantly, students use rich content, themes, and big ideas to learn history, geography, economics, civics, citizenship, and government. They also use important skills to “think like historians.” The Passports to Social Studies program units of study are guided by an essential question. The essential question is multi-faceted and is designed for open discussion and interpretation and can be applied beyond the content of one specific unit. Each lesson from the Passports to Social Studies program and Civics for All is guided by a focus question to direct students on developing the relevant skills, practices, and knowledge objectives that lead to historical understanding. Students are challenged to think like historians and encouraged to raise questions, think critically, consider many perspectives, and gather evidence in support of their interpretations through the practice of chronological processing, decision-making, and historical research and analysis. These real- world skills will serve students well as participating citizens of a democracy and as productive adults in the future. Passports are translated to Chinese and Spanish, and we follow the 50 – 50 model for our dual language programs. P.S. 94 is a Civics for All partner school. Civics for All is a companion to the Passport to Social Studies, designed with the New York State K-12 Social Studies Framework, the New York State Next Generation English Language Arts & Literacy in History/Social Studies Learning Standards, and the New York City K-12 Social Studies Scope & Sequence in mind. Our teachers translate students' resources and instructional materials to Spanish and Chinese to teach our students in the dual-language programs. The Civics for All initiative strengthens opportunities for students to develop skills and knowledge related to democracy. The goal is for all our students to become engaged citizens who can think critically and are empowered to chart the course that our city and country will take. Civics for All helps schools support students in making connections between history, current events, and democratic structures. Civics for All lessons are incorporated into Passports to Social Studies units and lessons to cultivate civic responsibility and awareness in our students to become active and informed participants of our democratic society. The Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework from Cultivating Genius by Dr. Gholdy Muhammad is a universal teaching and learning model that helps teachers cultivate the genius within students and within themselves and teach in ways that create space for mutual empowerment, confidence, and self-reliance. The Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework is an equity framework that believes every time children of color come together to read, write, think, and learn, they are making sense of who they are through Identity, developing their proficiencies in the content they are learning within through Skills, becoming smarter about something or gaining new knowledge and concepts in the world through Intellect, and developing the ability to read texts to understand power, authority and anti-oppression through Criticality. These five goals or pursuits are connected to the body of literacy research on cognitive and sociocultural perspectives of literacy development and what youth need to attain personal and academic success: Identity: It is composed of who we are, who others say we are, and who we desire to be. Youth need opportunities in school to explore multiple facets of selfhood, but also learn about the identities of others who may differ. Knowing about the DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 227 of 304 cultures of other people teaches them how to respect, love, and live in harmony with others who don’t look or know the world as they do. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction help students to learn something about themselves and/or about others?” Skills: It is essential for today’s youth to read, write, and think about diverse sets of literature in multimodal forms in order to develop their literacy skills. It is important that students develop the skills necessary in not only literacy but in all content areas. In particular, the skill of writing development should be cultivated in each content area because it entails the act of reading something, thinking about the content, considering new ideas to set on paper, and then communicating those words to an intended audience. Skills give across areas of learning students access and tools for education. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction build students’ skills for the content area?” Intellect: Intellect or knowledge is what we learn or understand about various topics, concepts, and paradigms. Through the pursuit of Identity and Skills, the possibility is created for Intellect, and when students develop intellectualism, they can express their ideas, work through justice-centered solutions to the world’s problems and expand their mental capacities. Intellect is not only about academics, but also emotional intelligence and self-and social awareness. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction build students’ knowledge and mental powers?” Criticality: It is the capacity to read, write, and think in ways of understanding how power, privilege, social justice, equity, and oppression operate throughout society particularly for populations who have been historically marginalized in the world. It enables students to question both the world and texts within it to better understand the truth in history, power, and equity. Teaching criticality humanizes instruction and makes it more compassionate. It begins to give students the tools they need to respond to injustice in and around schools and to tell the difference between facts and truths. As an educator, ask yourself, “How will my instruction engage students’ thinking about power and equity and the disruption of oppression?” Joy: This is another aspect of the framework because it’s essential to maintain one’s journey. All the pursuits are collaborative and encourage others to participate. When we offer a variety of subjects to excite students’ interest, bring joy and pleasure from reading, and stimulate their minds to think and debate about significant issues, we create an environment that affords students the opportunity to shape their own ideas through acts of literacy. It is our job as educators to not just teach skills, but teach students to know, validate, & celebrate who they are. As an educator, ask yourself, “How am I elevating beauty in humanity?” By implementing Passports to Social Studies, Civics for All, Social-Emotional CASEL Competencies, and the Historically Responsive Literacy (HRL) Framework we will prepare our students to be productive citizens of a global community and to nurture and promote global awareness. For our students to be globally aware citizens, students must understand and be able to: connect the local and the global, understanding of how the actions of people around the planet have an economic, technological, and cultural influence on all peoples of the world. participate in local and global economies. be open-minded, especially in understanding one’s own cultural lens as well as others’ distinct cultural lenses. celebrate similarities among different groups of people. understand and respect peoples’ differences. use electronic technologies in order to research people and cultures in every world region. understand the importance of cross-cultural communication, both within the United States and across borders. recognize and reduce stereotypes and prejudices. have compassion for all peoples of the world. assume responsibility for the ways in which they process information and read the world with a critical eye, refusing to accept unexamined information as factual or truth. This school year Passports to Social Studies and Civics for All has been integrated with Social Emotional CASEL Competencies. As students engage in historical explorations, global perspectives, historical literacies, and cultural studies, they will be able to deepen their connections and strengthen their personal social emotional skills in the areas of self-awareness, self-management, social awareness, relationships skills, and responsible decision making. This year Social Studies units of study maps will continue to have an essential question along with a social emotional focus question aligned to the content as well as SEL competency. Students will have multiple opportunities throughout a unit to revisit and enhance social emotional skills using complimentary lessons from Civics for All, and HEART resources such as Everyday Circle Cards, Humane Education Resource Guide, Kindness for All Guide, and the R.U.L.E.R Mood Meter. Mathematics Assessments All Eureka Math2 assessments are considered formative because they are intended to inform instruction. The assessments may also be considered summative when you choose to use the data to produce a grade or report. On its own, a single assessment does not show a complete picture of student progress. For example, a short assessment might use a single question to assess student understanding of part of a standard, thus producing a limited perspective. Use a combination of observational and scored assessments to understand and report on overall student performance. The following images show the various assessments that are included in the Eureka Math2 core curriculum. As the images suggest: Exit Tickets are DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 228 of 304 designed for use at the end of each lesson. A Topic Ticket or Topic Quiz replaces the Exit Ticket in the final lesson of each topic. Make intentional choices about when to give Topic Tickets. You might give them right after teaching the lessons in the topic or wait a day or two as students internalize their recent learning. The Module Assessment is typically given when module instruction is complete. Exit Tickets - Grades 1-5 Exit Tickets are short assessments that close lessons. These assessments use at least one problem or question to assess whether a student has learned the basic skills and concepts needed for success in upcoming lessons. Items reflect the minimum that students must demonstrate to meet the lesson objective. Students complete Exit Tickets independently, with directions or problems read aloud as necessary. In a typical classroom, most students with basic understanding can finish within 3–5 minutes. In some settings, honoring the timeframe is more important than requiring students to finish. For example, a student’s inability to finish within 5 minutes may be valuable information. In other settings, you may extend the time to allow all students to finish as appropriate. Students with disabilities must be provided testing accommodations as per the IEP/504 when teachers administer classroom assessments. Topic Tickets - Grades 1-2 A Topic Ticket replaces the Exit Ticket in the final lesson of each topic. Topic Tickets are short sets of items that assess proficiency with the major concepts and skills from the topic. Many items allow students to show evidence of one or more of the Standards for Mathematical Practice (MPs). You may use the Standards and Achievement Descriptors at a Glance charts to find which MPs you may be more likely to see from your students on a given assessment item in relation to the content that is assessed. For example, you may be likely to see evidence of MP1, MP2, MP4, MP5, and MP6 on Module 1 Topic A Quiz as those are the MPs explicitly identified in the lessons of that topic. Students complete Topic Tickets independently, with directions or problems read aloud as necessary. However, typical items on analogous versions are not clones of the original version. Use the analogous versions to give retakes, with reteaching or additional practice between takes, until students score proficient or above. Newcomers During the intake process newcomers and their families are given a tour of the school. Parent coordinators meet with parents and share community resources and assess the needs the families may have so we can address their need of support. When a newcomer first arrives in the classroom, they are partnered strategically with a learning partner that will support his/hers/their transition. They help them get familiar with our building as well as the classroom flow of the day and student resources. Newcomers students receive instructional support in the program they are enrolled in: ENL or dual language. Additionally, they are invited to participate in our English language arts after school program designed to develop vocabulary and address early reading strategies. They are also invited to participate in our weekend program focused on developing scaffolding strategies and vocabulary focus. Additionally, we also invite them to participate in our immigrant-newcomers theater after school program where they can explore the arts as they develop their understanding of the English language. What supports does your school provide to ELLs with IEPs to achieve their IEP goals and attain English language proficiency within the least restrictive environment? The first and most important step to promoting the academic growth of our ELL’s with IEP’s is by promoting a growth mindset among our staff and students. Without first developing the belief that these students can and will achieve their IEP goals any other curricular, instructional, and scheduling modifications are viewed pejoratively rather than restorative and necessary actions that promote equity and access to our most vulnerable student population. The IEP’s of our differently abled ELL’s are written by classroom teachers and service providers with the guidance of an IEP teacher and finalized by the assistant principal in charge of SWD’s. By engaging a team of educators in writing these IEP’s, each student’s goals, recommendations, task, assessment and testing modifications are developed holistically and collaboratively, ensuring that each IEP is inclusive and reflective of the whole child including their social, emotional, physical, linguistic and academic needs. Once the IEP is in place, the student’s needs are clearly communicated to service providers, cluster teachers and other staff as necessary. Every effort is made to educate the student in the least restrictive environment and maintain the same level of quality and rigor as our students without IEP’s. We accomplish this by maintaining a level of flexibility when it comes to programming, task modification, and lesson delivery. One example is that our paraprofessionals are present for many of the same professional development sessions our teachers attend; this allows them to fully engage their students with an expectation for the same level of academic excellence as the classroom teacher. Additionally, the testing accommodations written in the IEP’s are considered when creating formative and summative assessments throughout the school year. These testing accommodations are also reflected in both formal and informal task modification in each content area. Furthermore, our ELL’s with IEP’s receive all the same technological and instructional supports as our ELL’s without IEP’s, this ensures that no matter the environment, we are enabling our students with the skills, strategies, and knowledge to achieve their IEP goals and English language proficiency. 6. How is home language assessed in each program model (DLBE, TBE, and ENL)? The ELLs that are identified as in need of related services receive them as prescribed by their Individualized Educational Plan (IEP). Those students that do not have an IEP are reviewed by the Response to Intervention Team (RTI), where an educational action plan is developed based on age, grade, and proficiency levels. The action plans are reviewed every 6 weeks to determine its success and to create any necessary revisions. Home language support is provided throughout the day during content areas such science, social studies and mathematics. Teachers provide support through the analysis of their language proficiency levels and through the home language arts progression to provide the proper scaffolds. We support our ELLs with specific materials to support second language acquisition. We all follow the SIOP model. All Dual language and ENL classes in grades K-5 use Rosetta Stones, and Raz Kids. Curriculum materials such as maps, charts, posters, and videos are available in English as well as in other languages. Manipulatives for mathematics and science instruction are also used. In addition, our SETSS teacher uses SPIRE for intervention to support our ELLS during one-to-one intervention in English. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 232 of 304 All classrooms have: • Culturally responsive classroom libraries that include books and other printed materials • Word Wall with pictures in the lower grades and content vocabulary with pictures in all grades • Multilingual and multicultural libraries • Listening Centers • Strategy and language charts related to current unit of study in Reading, Writing and Math • Charts modeling correct language usage • Flow of the Day with pictures • Computers • Laptops • IPads In addition, all classrooms are equipped with Smart Boards and Document cameras to enhance visual aspects of learning for ELLs. Our enrichment team created and designed a STEM lab that contains learning centers including a technology center that have tasks with language strategies and modifications to promote learning in English and the native language. Home language support is offered to our Dual Language and ENL students throughout the day through the curriculum, materials and interventions. Dual Language teachers support home language development by teaching literacy in the home language. Home language support is provided through all components of the Science of Reading. The Dual Language program hires teachers who are home language speakers or fluent speakers to reinforce ELLs mastery of their first language. All students, starting in Kindergarten, take books home in the home language to read independently. Our ENL teachers support home language development by providing books in home language for students to take home and read with their families. Home language dictionaries, glossaries and text books are also in every classroom. We strongly believe in respecting and recognizing the importance of language and culture and are fully committed to preparing our students to become citizens of the world. Students are taught according to their age and grade level. All ELL students receive required services and resources corresponding to their age, grade level and proficiency level. 7. (For grades 6-12 only) What language electives are offered to MLs/ELLs? How is your school preparing MLs/ELLs to obtain the New York State Seal of Biliteracy? N/A 8. Describe systems and structures for supporting co-teaching and collaboration among teachers for all ELL program models available at your school (e.g. integrated co-teaching, Dual Language Bilingual Education teacher partnerships, etc.). At PS 94, we are committed to fostering an inclusive, linguistically responsive learning environment through structured collaboration and co-teaching practices in both our Dual Language Bilingual Education (DLBE) and Integrated Co-Teaching (ICT) classrooms. Our systems are designed to support effective instruction, shared planning, and reflective practice to meet the diverse needs of all students, including English Language Learners (ELLs). Leadership strategically builds collaborative planning time into the master schedule to ensure that co- teachers—including general education, special education, ENL, and bilingual educators—have regular, protected time to plan together. This allows teams to align instructional goals, language objectives, and differentiation strategies that address the academic and linguistic needs of ELLs. Administrators and instructional coaches monitor the use and outcomes of this planning time through attendance tracking, planning documentation reviews, and follow-up conversations. In Dual Language classrooms, partner teachers co-plan weekly to align language allocation, content, and biliteracy instruction following the DLBE model. This allows teams to align instructional goals, language objectives, and differentiation strategies that address the academic and linguistic needs of ELLs. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 233 of 304 This ensures balanced language development and content mastery in both languages. In ICT classrooms, general education and special education teachers engage in co-planning focused on differentiation, scaffolding strategies for ELLs, and leveraging co-teaching models (e.g., station teaching, parallel teaching) to maximize engagement and support, and differentiate instruction within inclusive settings. Teachers participate in grade-level and vertical PLCs facilitated by instructional coaches and administrators, with a focus on best practices in curriculum planning, language development, culturally responsive pedagogy, and co-teaching strategies. DLBE teachers meet as a cross-grade team to align instructional practices and ensure continuity and coherence of the bilingual program. Teams use shared digital planning tools (e.g., Google Docs, common lesson templates) and follow data protocols to analyze NYSESLAT scores, formative assessments, and classroom data to inform targeted instruction. Instructional coaches support teaching teams through a cycle of co-planning, co-teaching, and feedback. Coaches work side- by-side with teachers to model high-leverage instructional practices, implement language scaffolds, and support student engagement strategies tailored for ELLs. Coaches also assist teams in using data to inform instruction, reflect on lesson effectiveness, and identify next steps for student support. Through these comprehensive systems of leadership, coaching, and collaborative structures, PS 94 ensures that co-teaching teams are empowered and equipped to deliver rigorous, inclusive, and language-rich instruction to meet the needs of all learners, particularly multilingual students. Feedback loops are maintained between teachers, coaches, and administrators to review student data, adjust instruction, and reflect on co-teaching roles and responsibilities. Additionally, school-wide professional development aligns with expectations for collaborative instruction and language development. 9. Describe how your overall, annual professional learning plan ensures that all staff – including administrators, teachers and -non-pedagogical staff – incorporates learning specifically related to the academic and social emotional needs of MLs/ELLs. Professional learning topics might include co- teaching strategies, or integrating language and content instruction. It is important to note that the culture and linguistic make-up of the staff at PS 94, from our administration, all the way to our paraprofessionals and non-pedagogical staff is as diverse as that of our students. This is an intentional hiring practice to ensure that a majority of our staff, both pedagogues and support staff such as Parent Coordinators and Secretaries, are multilingual. This is extremely valuable as they can communicate with parents and students directly, no matter the English language proficiency level. This promotes a school culture that validates and celebrates our ML’s/ELL’s from the moment they enter our school building. Additionally, our staff understands the perspective of the English Language Learner, as most of them have been in the shoes of a language learner at some point in their life. The identity and background of our staff merely set the stage for the professional learning plan we have put in place to address the needs of our students. At least 50% of all professional development, staff meetings, and professional learning opportunities are dedicated to the academic, social, and emotional needs of ML’s/ELL’s. Our whole staff PD’s include opportunities for our staff to reflect on their own experiences and background as it relates to education. Additionally, our whole group staff development is designed to shift the mindset around Social Emotional Learning and Equity education so that it is considered as a prerequisite to any content and academic instruction rather than an additional subject to “fit in”. At PS 94, every staff member is given the opportunity to engage in deep discussions about best practices in promoting the intellectual and emotional development of our students and each other. Equity, social justice, and advocacy is another area of our professional learning plan that directly relates to the needs of our ML’s/ELL’s. Our staff spends time dissecting and analyzing the inequities faced by our students in the greater societal context for the purpose of disrupting and dismantling existing systems that marginalize and oppress different groups of people, including the education system. At the grade and department level, our professional learning centers around how we can align our instructional practice to the specific academic skill needs of our students. We also spend this time aligning our PBL and content areas to the greater school culture and vision for an equitable education that addresses emotional and intellectual development. We spend grade level meetings engaged in inquiry that is specific to the developmental needs of that particular grade band; for example, what content and skill knowledge is necessary to prepare students to write letters to local government persuading them to address the lack of multilingual signs in their local park. This DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 234 of 304 two pronged approach to professional learning, whole staff conversations about culture, language, diversity, and equity as well as grade and department inquiry into specific academic content and skill needs, ensures that our entire staff is working towards a common goal of meeting the academic, and social emotional needs of our ML’s/ELL’s. To ensure that all teachers, paraprofessionals and related service providers including speech, guidance, psychologist, OT, PT, SETSS and others teaching ELLs are familiar with second language strategies we provide targeted professional development on both the theory and practice of second language acquisition frequently. Due to our large ELL population we are all collectively responsible for student achievement and we ensure that everyone is familiar with best practices for ELLs. Professional development is provided through weekly grade meetings, staff conferences, one to one modeling and use of Lab site classrooms. We have coaches and teacher leaders that provide professional development to all staff. Our Dual Language Coaches and Assistant Principals provide specific professional development to dual language teachers. Teachers participate in Brooklyn North series of workshops targeting instruction for ELLs and then turnkey to the rest of the staff. Additionally staff receive professional development to ensure all pedagogues provide language support for our ELLs in all content areas and subjects. Teachers assist ELLs as they transition in various ways. In May, teachers prepare articulation notes and students visit the grade they are moving up to, they have the opportunity to talk to teachers and students in the grade and are told of the expectations. Grade 5 teachers visit middle schools with their students and our Guidance Counselor assists in transitioning to middle school through workshops and one to one counseling for both parents and students. Students in Grades 3, 4, and 5 will take part in an exciting Career Day event designed to inspire curiosity, broaden horizons, and connect classroom learning to real-world opportunities. Through engaging presentations and interactive sessions, students will meet professionals from a variety of fields, such as healthcare, technology, education, public service, the arts, and skilled trades. In addition, the student council serves as ambassadors for our school and represent us at community forums and at recruitment fairs. Our Parent Coordinators also create and provide parent workshops for parents of ELLs related to strategies to support work at home, Next Generation Language Standards support and amongst many providing technological resources to support ELL language development. According to our Professional Development Calendar, we are providing professional development in all content areas geared to understanding and providing appropriate support for ELLs in mathematics, reading, writing, science and SEL. 10. Describe your school’s professional development plan in order to meet the mandated Continuing Teacher and Leader Education (CTLE) ELL specific hours for certification renewal as per CR Part 154 (15% of total hours for all certified faculty [holders of professional certificates in the classroom teaching service, educational leadership service and level III teaching assistant certificate holders] and 50% of total hours for bilingual education/ENL teachers receive ELL-specific professional development). At the beginning of the school year, PS 94 Cabinet (Principal and Assistant Principals) analyze the MOSL data, as well as, the overall rating of teachers to identify the professional development needs of the staff for the school year. Additionally, we use the results of the NYSESLAT and State Tests and reflect on the students' scores and the support teachers are going to need to improve their teaching practice to effectively target instruction and supports for our English language learners at PS 94. Additionally, during the Initial Planning Conferences, administrators discuss all data (students' and teacher's) with each teacher and work on identifying priority areas of improvement together. Finally, our Professional Development committee works with the staff and administration to align professional development sessions with the ELL language development needs of our school. The professional development committee meets and makes recommendations regarding professional development for the teachers to the Principal, together we ensure that the focus of the professional development teachers receive at PS 94 is in accordance with the CR-Part 154 requirements {15% ELL-Specific professional development sessions for all teachers and 50% Ell-specific professional development session for all ENL/Bilingual teachers). The Professional development plan (including agenda and attendance) is in the Principal's office and is revised monthly to adjust the sessions to the needs of our school. Which screening assessments (e.g.iReady, MAP Growth, Acadience, or STAR Reading) does your school use to guide instructional planning for your ELLs? Up until school year 2022-2023, our school administered and studied ELA and Math data using iReady K-5 in ELA and Math. For the school year 2025-2026, PS 94 will be administering Acadience Reading and Math Screener for grades K-2 and Map Reading and Math Screener for grades 3-5. PS 94 will continue to administer iReady diagnostics in Reading and Math as they provide a digital path of intervention. For Grades 3-5 students that need Phonics based instruction, an additional screener of iReady (phonological awareness and phonics) will be administered during the periodic assessment windows to monitor their progress). Considerations will be made to assess them using EOG summative assessments or Acadience. In addition to the annual data provided by the iReady Reading grades K-5, iReady Mathematics grades 3-5, NYSITELL, NYSESLAT, and State Tests in ELA, Math, and Science, our school collects data on a daily, weekly and monthly basis, both formally and informally. Conference notes from small group instruction and whole group checks for understanding, as well as the social and emotional data collected through use of Mood Meter check-ins throughout the day give teachers daily and weekly data about their students’ academic and social needs. This data also will help teachers and the SEL lead team to paint a vivid picture of the needs and strengths of our students from an SEL perspective as well as determining which CASEL competencies we can enhance through our curriculum and programming. Module pre and post assessments in Math, and end of module assessments in ELA end of module assessments also provide a complex picture of a student's needs on a month to month or biweekly basis. At the end of each module, students complete a formal writing task that serves as a summative assessment. Grade-Level Writing genres by module vary, but include: Narrative (personal narrative, story retell, fiction), Informative/Explanatory (how-to, research report, explanatory paragraph), Opinion/Argumentative (opinion letters, persuasive essays). These writing tasks measure how well students apply writing skills taught throughout the module (e.g., structure, content development, grammar, and conventions). After these summative assessments we provide staff with time to examine student work using rubrics that provide teachers with concrete data about their student’s strengths and areas for improvement. By facilitating teacher to teacher, grade level, and content area collaboration and data analysis on a monthly basis we have been able to guide instructional planning and specialized Tier 1 interventions for our ELL’s of every language proficiency. We firmly believe that collaboration is the key to quality curriculum alignment and modification; often our teachers find that there are similar subgroups of student needs across classrooms and by pooling time and resources, all those diverse needs can be met in real time. These deep dives into skills and strategies assessments allow administration the opportunity to reallocate resources such as instructional coaches, Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions and other instructional supports (eg. before and after school programs) . Furthermore these benchmark assessments and progress monitoring provide specific data and next steps for the in-house instructional coaches, MTSS and SETTS teachers who assist in Tier 2 and Tier 3 interventions so that they can collaborate with classroom teachers to hit the ground running. 12. For all grades, describe how your school uses data to guide instruction for ELLs within a Multi- Tiered System of Supports (MTSS). Refer to the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs and ELLs and MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs to help in the development and implementation of your school’s plan for MLs/ELLs. PS 94 uses a Multi-Tiered System of Supports (MTSS) to ensure that Multilingual Learners (MLs)/English Language Learners (ELLs) receive responsive, data-informed instruction aligned to both language development and academic progress. Using the Instructional Leadership Framework and the NYC MTSS Guide for MLs and ELLs, we take a DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 238 of 304 proactive and inclusive approach to meeting the needs of ELLs across all grade levels. Data Sources include iReady (Reading and/or Math) diagnostics, Acadience K-2 Screener (Reading and/or Math), Map 3-5 Screener (Reading or Math). For Grades 3-5 students that need Phonics based instruction, an additional screener of iReady (phonological awareness and phonics) will be administered during the periodic assessment windows to monitor their progress). Considerations will be made to assess them using EOG summative assessments or Acadience, HMH module assessments, NYSESLAT, formative classroom assessments, writing samples, and student work. Teachers deliver grade-level, Next Generation Learning Standards aligned to ELA instruction (e.g., HMH Into Reading), while embedding language objectives and scaffolds for ELLs. Tier 1 instruction is differentiated using student language proficiency data and guided by the Instructional Leadership Tool for MLs/ELLs, which emphasizes culturally and linguistically responsive practices. Teachers monitor language development across all four modalities (listening, speaking, reading, and writing) and adjust instruction accordingly. Tier 2 – Students identified through Tier 1 data receive small-group, targeted instruction focused on specific skill gaps (e.g., vocabulary, decoding, oral language). Research-based interventions such as Edwards Orton-Gillingham for structured literacy, and Rosetta Stone for oral language development, are used with fidelity. Teachers collaborate with ENL staff and MTSS providers to co-plan and analyze student progress every 4–6 weeks using MTSS tracking tools. Tier 3 instruction is Intensive, individualized Support. ELLs receiving Tier 3 services benefit from individualized intervention plans, targeted literacy instruction, and ongoing progress monitoring. Support may be intensified by increasing session frequency, reducing group size, and using home language support when appropriate. Teachers and support staff meet in ILT or MTSS team meetings to review data, adjust interventions, and involve families in decision-making. Data drives lesson planning, flexible grouping, and formative assessment strategies aligned with language and content standards. Teachers engage in professional learning on interpreting NYSESLAT scores, setting language goals, and delivering scaffolded instruction. ELLs receive scaffolded instruction to grade-level content while building English proficiency. The school uses data cycles (e.g., Data Inquiry Teams, ILT reviews) to: Identify trends across ELL subgroups (e.g., newcomers, SIFE, LTELLs), adjust curriculum pacing and intervention groupings, ensure equity in access to core instruction and enrichment. FORMATIVE 14. Which formative assessments (e.g. NYC performance Tasks, ELL Periodic Assessment, HLA Assessment, DRA, teacher-created assessments, etc.) are used to inform and drive instruction? While we have official tri-annual Acadience K-2 and Map 3-5 Screeners in ELA and Mathematics and the iReady diagnostic, most of our student data comes from our end of module assessments in ELA assessments, such as unit Pre and Post assessments for Math, Social Studies, and Science; as well as our formative assessments such as NYC performance tasks, ACADIENCE Reading and Math Screeners (K-2), Map Screener (3-5), iReady ELA and Math k-5, Exemplars, informal language progressions, HLA performance tasks, and PBL in each content area, including Social Emotional Learning. For Grades 3-5 students that need Phonics based instruction, an additional screener of iReady (phonological awareness and phonics) will be administered during the periodic assessment windows to monitor their progress). Considerations will be made to assess them using EOG summative assessments or Acadience. All of these formal and informal assessments combined, paint a complete picture of each student, including their interests and identities, their language proficiency, and academic content and skill level. Once we know our students well, teachers can plan instruction that engages and excites each learner. For example, a student who struggles with written expression in informational writing as determined by their writing pre-assessment can be given targeted small group instruction in writing about a topic they have background knowledge of and experience with as determined by their success with a prior PBL in STEM or Social Studies. Students who excel at logical thinking and reasoning as evidenced in their HLA performance task, but struggle to complete Exemplars in English can have additional linguistic support such as bilingual dictionaries, use of manipulatives and additional practice with English on Rosetta Stone. Our school is adept at adapting our instruction to meet the diverse needs of our students because we understand that diagnosing strengths, areas of improvement and then addressing the whole child is what leads to academic success. Grade leaders, grade teams, supervisors and instructional support teams use all data streams to support, intervene and track all ELLs students at risk and how they progress. This data informs our instructional plan by completing periodic assessments that track our students progress as well as the support we provide per content, writing genre and reading. As we meet for inquiry we use the student work to analyze trends, strengths and struggle to then inform our teacher practice and student learning. We assess early literacy skills in several ways at PS 94. Beginning the school year 2025-2026 we will also administer the ACADIENCE Reading and Mathematics Screener in grades K-2 that measures the acquisition of early literacy skills, as well as the MAP in reading and Mathematics for grades 3-5. The insights provided by these screeners highlight the need for foundational skills instruction in Grades K-2. As a result of the screener data, teachers are able to identify early foundational skills to develop tailored individualized instruction in order to set students on a path to proficiency. We also administer the iReady diagnostic test in grades K-5 in English Language Arts and Mathematics. The insights provided by these assessments highlight and identify ELLs who are at risk and are in need of receiving additional literacy support. Student writing pieces are assessed using the HMH Writing Rubrics and the the English Language Arts 2-Point and 4-Point New York State Writing Evaluation Rubric. End of unit projects (using the Project Based Learning approach) are also assessed in the areas of presentation and collaboration. Teachers have created progress logs to track oral language development aligned to the Speaking and Listening standards. The insights provided by this assessment highlight the oral language progress and needs of ELLs. Teachers adapt and design activities that allow learners to practice single modalities (listening, speaking , reading, writing) as well as linked modalities (e.g., listening and DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 240 of 304 speaking, reading and writing). Teachers continue to include grammar practice in their instruction while working toward the goal of global proficiency. Through the PBLs, students work together cooperatively, they have to speak and listen to each other and are frequently engaged in synthesizing information from sources (reading), taking notes (writing) and pulling together their ideas (writing) for later presentation (speaking/listening). Teachers also use progress logs document data on students in the four modalities. In addition, presentation rubrics in PBL encompass the 4 modalities of language by having students explain their ideas and present their information; organize their ideas in both writing and speaking; presenting their project individually or collaboratively in a group to an audience, and responding to audience questions. In addition, teachers have created assessments in Chinese and Spanish in reading and writing to assess literacy skills for our Chinese and Spanish Speaking population. These assessments are aligned to the next generation learning standards. We assess each student's writing vocabulary as a way of tracking vocabulary development. In grades K-2 we use the results of these early literacy assessments to identify ELLs who are at risk and these students participate in additional small group instruction with targeted learning activities, Chinese Language, Spanish Language Intervention or English as a New Language Intervention. This school year 2025-2026 we administered the Home Language New York City Performance Tasks in Chinese and Spanish to students in Kindergarten through fifth grade in the Dual Language programs, as well as, the Chinese reading test for Dual Language students in grades 3-5. The Chinese Reading Test is used to assess students' development of Chinese literacy. This test will be used to meet Commissioner’s Regulations Part 154 evaluation requirements relating to progress in home language arts. The Chinese Language Arts (CLA) NYC Performance Tasks (NYCPTs) provide teachers with information about their students’ strengths and needs in their Chinese literacy skills and on students’ language development. These assessments are administered 2-3 times during the school year. Teachers will use the assessment data to provide targeted skills based instruction, and to provide opportunities for students to transfer literacy skills between their home language and new language. The Spanish Language Arts (SLA) NYC Performance Tasks (NYCPTs) provide teachers with information about their students’ strengths and needs in their Spanish literacy skills and n students’ language development. These assessments are administered two to three times during the school year. Teachers will use the assessment data to provide targeted skills based instruction, and to provide opportunities for students to transfer literacy skills between their home language and new language. 15. Include: Topics discussed during these meetings How your school ensures families receive necessary translation/interpretation supports to meaningfully engage in the meetings How your school schedules these meetings to facilitate attendance, and how your school maintains evidence of these meetings as required per the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs. a. ELL Program Orientation to inform parents/guardians of newly identified ELLs of ELL program options, including the program goals and requirements for all three ML/ELL program models: Dual Language Bilingual Education, Transitional Bilingual Education and English as a New Language regardless of whether the school currently has either type of bilingual program and provide a high- quality orientation section on the Next Generation Learning Standards, assessments, and school expectations for English Language Learners. When parents first come to register their children, PS 94’s ELL Intake Team—consisting of a licensed pedagogue, an Assistant Principal, the Parent Coordinator, and a secretary—meets with each family to complete the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS). In compliance with CR Part 154, this survey is always administered by a licensed and trained pedagogue, who conducts the mandated in-person interview with the parent and student to determine the child’s home language and NYSITELL eligibility. Once the HLIS process is complete, families participate in the required ELL Parent Orientation. During this orientation, the team provides a high-quality overview of: The three ELL program models available (Dual Language Bilingual Education, Transitional Bilingual Education, and English as a New Language), including each program’s goals, instructional model, and benefits. PS 94 currently offers Dual Language Programs and ENL programs. Next Generation Learning Standards, expectations for academic progress, and the assessments that monitor English language development (NYSITELL and NYSESLAT). Language rights, interpretation and translation services, and parent options to select or transfer between ELL program models. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 243 of 304 We also share how PS 94 integrates ELL instruction within its dual-language and ENL programs to support multilingualism and academic rigor. To ensure equitable participation, interpretation and translation are provided in all major school languages (Spanish, Mandarin, Cantonese, and English). Documents, surveys, and consent forms are distributed in families’ preferred language, as required by DOE policy. When needed, a qualified interpreter/translator—as defined in CR Part 154—is available for additional language support The orientation takes place in-person when families register and is scheduled flexibly during school hours, with evening and virtual options offered during the year to ensure maximum access. Parents are encouraged to ask questions and review sample bilingual materials. To maintain evidence of compliance, PS 94: Keeps a visitor logbook signed by attending parents. Maintains agendas and sign-in sheets from each orientation. Retains copies of translated materials and parent program selection forms in students’ cumulative folders. Uploads documentation of ELL parent meetings into ATS and STARS as part of the annual compliance verification process. Additionally, the Assistant Principal or Principal meets with each family to confirm placement and enter data in ATS, ensuring the student is enrolled in the appropriate bilingual or ENL program. Families also receive their Parent Notification and Program Agreement Letters in their preferred language and are invited to a follow-up orientation at the beginning of the school year to review program progress and supports. In summary: PS 94 ensures full compliance with CR Part 154 by conducting all required orientations with fidelity—led by licensed pedagogues, supported by interpreters, and documented through signed logs, agendas, and program agreements. Teachers follow the expectations of the meetings as communicate them with our staff below: Parent Teacher Conference 5: Any day during the school year only for ELLs (mandated). Agenda, attendance and must be shared with Assistant Principals Rose Rodriguez and Sharon Valentin. Information should be included in the “ELEMENTARY SCHOOL CUMMULATIVE RECORD personal and Educational data” form located inside the cumulative folder for each student. Additional information regarding the Mandatory PTC conference for parents of ELLs, or MLLs students: Each year, schools are responsible for holding annual one-on-one meetings with the parents or guardians of all English Language Learner (ELL) students enrolled in either an English as a New Language (ENL) or bilingual class setting. As per Commissioner’s Regulation (CR) Part 154, these meetings must be held at least once a year to discuss the goals of the ELL program, the child's language development progress, English language proficiency assessment results, and language development needs across all content areas. These meetings are in addition to regularly scheduled parent-teacher conferences, initial parent orientations, and quarterly progress meetings. Schools must schedule these meetings at convenient times to ensure attendance and provide a qualified interpreter or translator in the language or mode of communication the parent or guardian best understands. These meetings should not be held during regularly scheduled parent-teacher conferences and should involve a deep review of student work and a discussion about language acquisition and progress toward English proficiency. They can occur virtually, but not over the phone. You have the option of a “teams” or “zoom” meeting, all documentation must be provided and kept in the student’s cumulative folder. Schools must ensure these meetings are scheduled and completed by the end of the school year. What are the titles of the members of your ELL identification team? Note: Members of the ELL identification team must be pedagogues. PS 94 ELL Identification team: Principal, Assistant Principal, Language Allocation Coordinator, ENL teacher, Instructional Coach/Dual language teacher, secretary PS 94 Language Proficiency Team (LPT)-(as part of the intake team) Assistant Principal Special Education, Special Education IEP teacher, the student's parent or guardian Identification Process for English Language Learners All students who enroll after the first day of school, must be identified and placed within the standard 10 school days of enrollment (or 20 school days for incoming students with IEPs). The ELL identification process includes: 1. In-person administration of the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) by qualified personnel (licensed and trained pedagogue) which includes a mandated interview with the student and parent to determine the student's home language. 2. If a student has an IEP, the Language Proficiency Team (LPT) should determine NYSITELL eligibility and complete the NYSITELL determination form. 3. Administration of the NYSITELL to eligible students. 4. Sending parents/guardians notifications of the NYSITELL results and entitlement/non-entitlement of services in their preferred language. 5. Administration of the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (Spanish LAB) to newly identified ELLs whose home language is Spanish. 6. Parent orientation and completion of Parent Survey and Program Agreement form. 7. Place the student into an ELL program based on NYSITELL results. 2. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 249 of 304 At P.S. 94, we follow the NYC DOE’s guideline for identifying MLLs/ELLs throughout the year, making sure that this process integrates smoothly with our instructional programs. Potential ELLs - The administration of the HLIS by qualified personnel, ELL identification team - Mandated document individual interview with the student and parent to determine the student’s home language, including a review of the student’s abilities - Determination of eligibility to take the NYSITELL - For incoming students with IEP, this includes convening LPTs and completion of the NYSITELL determination form - Administration of the NYSITELL to eligible students - Administration of the Spanish LAB to newly identified ELLs whose home language is Spanish - Parent notification and parent orientation in their preferred language - Placement into ELL program Former ELLs - Students who exited services based on their Spring 2025 NYSESLAT scores and those students who met the exception of an Expanding level on the 2025 NYSESLAT and a level 3 or 4 on the grades 3-8 NYS ELA exam will continue to be entitled to ENL instruction as per CR Part 154. Current ELLs - Schedule ELLs for ENL based on spring NYSESLAT results and other available data. The ELL identification team has specific roles during the process: 1. The pupil Accounting Secretary and a Parent Coordinator welcome the family when they first enter our main office. The Pupil Accounting Secretary informs the LAP Coordinator and an assistant principal about the intake. Then, a trained language pedagogue meets with the family and support the family in completing the Home Language Identification Survey (HLIS) by qualified personnel (licensed and trained pedagogue) which includes a mandated interview with the student and parent to determine the student's home language. 2. If a student has an IEP, the Language Proficiency Team (LPT) should determine NYSITELL eligibility and complete the NYSITELL determination form. The committee is formed by an Assistant Principal, a pedagogue, another pedagogue with a special education certificate and the parent who together will recommend eligibility to take the NYSESLAT to the principal. 3. Once the student is registered, an ENL teacher will conduct the administration of the NYSITELL to eligible students. 4. The assistant principal will then send parents/guardians notifications of the NYSITELL results and entitlement/non-entitlement of services in their preferred language. 5. If the student speaks Spanish we will proceed with the administration of the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (Spanish LAB) to newly identified ELLs whose home language is Spanish. The test will be administered by a dually certified in Spanish teacher. 6. The parent will then meet with an Assistant Principal and we will finish the parent orientation and completion of Parent Survey and Program Agreement form. 7. Place the student into an ELL program based on NYSITELL results. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 250 of 304 Principal Certification In accordance with New York State’s Commissioner’s Regulations Part 154 as outlined and implemented in the NYC DOE Policy and Reference Guide for MLs/ELLs, I, attest that the following ELL identification procedures and activities are adhered to, and staff are trained on the items listed below. The ELL Identification Process is completed for each student within 10 school days of enrollment (20 school days for students entering with IEPs). Enrollment status of each newly admitted student is determined: a. If a student has been in New York State public schools within the past 2 years, our school contacts the previous school to obtain ELL status, relevant assessment scores, and English language proficiency level. b. If a student has been outside of New York State public schools for more than 2 years, our school administers the ELL Identification Process as part of reentry. The home language of the student is determined by a trained and licensed pedagogue. a. The parent completes the Home Language Identification Survey in the parent’s preferred language; translation services are provided by a bilingual staff member, the DOE’s over-the-phone translation services, or an alternative, high quality translation service. An interview with parents and students in English and the home language is conducted. This includes reviewing documents, prior assessments, and academic experience. An assessment of the student’s abilities and/or work samples determines the student’s literacy and math level in his/her home language. Eligibility for the NYSITELL is determined. a. If the newly enrolled student is entering with an IEP, the school’s Language Proficiency Team (LPT) recommends eligibility to the principal, who obtains final approval from the superintendent. Students are administered the NYSITELL, if eligible. Notification letters are sent to the parent/guardian in their preferred language. a. Parent is notified of their child’s ELL status and results of the NYSITELL. If the student is identified as an ELL and the student’s home language is Spanish, the student is administered the Spanish Language Assessment Battery (LAB). Student with Interrupted/Inconsistent Formal Education (SIFE) status is determined using New York State’s resources. If a student is a newly identified ELL, the parent is invited to the parent orientation meeting. a. The parent orientation meeting provides parents/guardians with an explanation of each ELL program model offered in NYC; the parent video describing the three programs is presented (in parent’s preferred language); parents/guardians are encouraged to ask questions so that they are DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 251 of 304 informed about all ELL programs; translation and interpretation services are provided throughout the meeting. ELL is placed in the ELL program that the parent selected. a. If the bilingual program that the parent selected is not available, the parent is offered a transfer to a school with that bilingual program in accordance with NYC DOE transfer policies. b. If the ELL remains in the school, the ELL is placed in English as a New Language and our school keeps track of parent selection so that when minimum thresholds are met, we can open the ELL program of choice. Parents are notified that they have 45 days to appeal the ELL status of their child (ELL Re-identification Process). If the ELL Re-identification Process is implemented for any student, the principal reviews the decision of any student whose ELL status was changed because of the ELL Re-identification Process within 6 to 12 months. Copies of documentation regarding ELL identification, including letters and parent notifications are kept in the students’ cumulative files. To this end, schools must review and complete the annual LTI Plan to address its language assistance needs consistent with the requirements of this regulation. Identification of Critical Communications and standing, in-person meetings throughout the school year that require language assistance services and how the school plans to respond to these needs. Methods for the school to solicit and implement feedback on its provision of language assistance services. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 263 of 304 Title III Outline Title III Outline Title III Supplemental Program for ELLs for SY 2025-26 REQUIREMENTS Title III supplemental services for English Language Learners (ELLs) must include the following three components: Direct instruction activities must be used to support language development, English and home language instruction, high academic achievement in math, and/or high academic achievement in other core academic areas. The Title III supplemental instructional services must be based on student need. These supplemental services should complement core bilingual and ENL services required under CR Part 154. Direct supplemental services should be provided for before school, after school, and Saturday programs. Teachers providing the services must be bilingual education and/or English for Speakers of Other Languages (ESOL) certified teachers. High quality professional development that is "of sufficient intensity and duration to have a positive and lasting impact on the teachers’ performance in classrooms." Professional development activities should be well-planned, ongoing events rather than one-day or short-term workshops and conferences. Parent engagement and supports must ensure that there are appropriate translation and interpretation services to meet community needs. These are in addition to mandated activities, such as parent orientation during ELL identification process or annual ELL parent meetings. Note: The Title III ELL program planning ratio is as follows: 60% to direct instruction, 10% to professional development (if funded using Title III ELL funds), 10% to parent engagement (if funded using Title III ELL funds), and 20% to OTPS (if funded using Title III ELL funds). For more information on Title III requirements, please see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. Any updates or revisions to this plan must be made through the iPlan portal. Theatre Program-for up to 100 ELLs Students This theater program brings P.S. 94K’s vision to life. It reflects our mission to provide rigorous, culturally sustaining instruction while fostering multilingualism, multi-literacies, and student voice. By supporting identity, language, and creativity, the program affirms our commitment to nurturing confident, expressive, and empowered learners who will lead with empathy and pride in their heritage. P.S. 94K serves 371 ELLs, representing 49.21% of total enrollment. Of these, 79.8% are newcomers (0–3 years), highlighting a need for scaffolded, engaging opportunities to accelerate language development. This program directly responds to these needs. Through script analysis, dialogue practice, character exploration, and ensemble performance, students are immersed in an authentic, high-interest context where language use is purposeful, embodied, and emotionally resonant. These experiences promote fluency, expand vocabulary, and elevate engagement—all essential to academic success and personal growth. Theater provides students with numerous invaluable opportunities for connection. It also allows students to develop their English proficiency skills as they work together in reading, writing, listening, and speaking while engaged in the shared purpose of performing a play. Through the lens of theater, students connect to stories (literacy), peers, teachers, their emotions, and the world around them. These experiences cultivate language development, collaboration, reading and writing skills, and social relationships. By offering this program after school, students are given the time and space to explore and deepen these connections, leading to lasting learning experiences and positive identity development—especially for those new to the country. Within this after-school theater program, students will experience meaningful, multimodal engagement. They will: • Develop literacy skills by reading scripts as an ensemble • Memorize lines and songs, building vocabulary and fluency • Create original character bios and backstories, deepening comprehension • Explore acting and movement through kinesthetic learning • Reflect on characters’ emotional journeys, bridging fiction and real-life experiences Instructional strategies will include guided rehearsal, modeled reading, oral language scaffolding, and vocabulary instruction—all grounded in ENL best practices. Students will also participate in prop creation and basic stage design, engaging in project-based learning that integrates the arts with academic language acquisition. This experience is especially meaningful for ELLs, who will use all four modalities of language—reading, writing, listening, and speaking—while improving pronunciation, dialogue structure, and contextual understanding of vocabulary. Through performance, students will not only learn language—they will embody it, connect it to emotion, and bring it to life. Within an after-school theater program, the students will be able to connect their learning in practical, memorable, and life changing ways. Students will develop their literacy skills by working on a script, practice reading through the script as an ensemble, memorize lines and lyrics to songs, and even write down original character bios, objectives, and backstories. Through this process, there is constant reinforcement of language structures and vocabulary. The focus of supporting language development and peer collaboration for our students who are also English language learners is to provide them with enrichment opportunities that will increase engagement, and participation. For ELLs, this can be a significant experience because not only are they using all four modalities of language, they are also working on their speech, dialoguing techniques and acquisition of new vocabulary. The opportunity to build new vocabulary and knowledge of the English language throughout this process can provide lasting results because, after memorization, they have to create their character’s connection to DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 267 of 304 the story. In doing this, they are embodying the language and creating realistic characters who show action and emotions as they are telling the story. This requires a deeper level of understanding of the language and meaning of each word in a scene or song. By using these visual, auditory, reading, writing, and kinesthetic learning activities throughout the process, this program will innately reinforce students’ specific learning styles and greatly increase language learning. Once students have internalized their scripts, they will draw upon personal knowledge of emotions, relationships, and life events to portray believable characters. This step is especially powerful for ELLs navigating a new cultural landscape. It allows them to safely explore difficult or unfamiliar emotions, express their identities, and build empathy—all while gaining confidence in their English abilities. Students will have the opportunity to bring their prior life knowledge of relationships, emotions, situations, and the way people react to those situations to their character’s performance to communicate the story clearly to an audience. This is particularly important for students getting to know a new country. They will be able to share who they are and their past experiences as they make sense and make this country their new home. Deep understanding and connection to one’s own life experiences is crucial in developing believable characters onstage. Through fictional situations and characters, students can feel safe exploring relationships and emotions that they may otherwise feel insecure expressing. This safe space can teach students how to bring what they learn from the fictional characters and stories to real life situations, experiences, and emotional issues. Theater also demands ensemble collaboration. Students will build community as they work together to produce a culminating performance for the school and families. This inclusive and celebratory event reflects P.S. 94K’s commitment to cultivating creativity, nurturing compassion, and empowering multilingual students to lead and advocate. The students will also learn about theater production, creating all props and really understand what it takes to “ensemble” a play. Collaboration, sharing of ideas, building on others ideas, and working together as a team is at the core of every theater performance project. Students will have the opportunity to merge their cultures, ideas, and ways of communicating as they work together toward the final performance. The connections the students will make to each other as they become an ensemble will encourage them to seek out ways for further collaboration in every area of their lives. Indeed, they will realize that we are better together. They will conclude the program by performing to our school community and our families. For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program Theatre Program According to the 2024 EDAT, 51.7% of the student population at P.S. 94K are English Language Learners (422 out of DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 269 of 304 817 students), and 85.3% of those students are newcomers (0–3 years of service). These students are navigating both academic English and cultural adaptation. More than 41% of our ELL population exhibit early warning indicators —including limited classroom participation, low oral language use, and foundational literacy gaps—demonstrating a need for engaging, non-traditional supports that foster connection, confidence, and expression. Our Spring 2024 NYSESLAT data showed that the majority of students in grades 2–5 are struggling with productive modalities—particularly speaking and writing. Over 61.8% of ELLs did not meet expected English Language Proficiency (ELP) progress, with speaking and writing scores disproportionately represented in the lowest performance bands. This makes it essential to design extended day learning environments where language development is intentionally embedded into meaningful activities. As previously stated, this program intends to provide experiences for our families as they are making Sunset Park their new home. Our teachers' goal is primarily to provide opportunities for all to feel welcome as they learn together. In addition to using drama to develop our students’ language, drama also helps students share their emotions and develop peer-to-peer connections. They are encouraged adaptability, fluency, and communicative competence. It makes the need of using language positively as students are engaged successfully in real-life situations closely monitored by pedagogues to help them navigate the challenges of their new environment. During our theater program, students work and communicate together using all four modalities of language experientially in a welcoming and scaffolded environment. Vocabulary: Students will increase their vocabulary: through scripts, improv and scenery development, students will learn context based vocabulary as well as communication specific language and grammatical structures. Listening: Theater requires active listening. Students will have the opportunity to listen to teachers and peers while reading or discussing scenes and scripts. They will listen to how the English language sounds and will have the opportunity to practice while also speaking. Speaking: Theater will create many opportunities for students to verbally express themselves. Reading: Reading is a fundamental part of our theater program. Readalouds in groups as well as individually will support the classroom work and the skills and strategies learned during the day. Writing: Through script writing, students will learn to use their new vocabulary and language structures in this program. During the writing section of the program students will be able to improve their writing skills. Theater is an ideal response to this need. It naturally integrates all four modalities of language (listening, speaking, reading, and writing), while offering students a safe and affirming space to develop fluency, comprehension, and confidence. The structure of the program supports academic growth by: • Strengthening literacy skills through reading scripts, writing character backstories, analyzing story structure, and reflecting on narrative meaning. • Building oral language through line memorization, character rehearsal, and live performance in front of authentic audiences. • Promoting social-emotional growth as students explore emotions, relationships, and identity through character development and ensemble collaboration. Additionally, this program supports multicultural awareness and inclusive practice, encouraging ELLs to merge their personal stories and cultural backgrounds with fictional characters. In doing so, students gain a deeper connection to the English language and to one another. For many newcomers, theater becomes a vehicle to express who they are, where they come from, and how they see the world—strengthening both linguistic and personal identity. Basketball Program P.S. 94K serves a linguistically rich and growing multilingual population, with English Language Learners (ELLs) comprising 51.7% of the student body (422 out of 817 students). Of these, 85.3% are classified as newcomers (0–3 years of service), reflecting the urgent need to build foundational English language skills through high-interest, culturally responsive programs. An in-depth analysis of the Spring 2024 NYSESLAT results reveals that a significant percentage of ELLs continue to struggle with oral language development and written expression: • 61.8% of ELLs did not meet their expected English Language Proficiency (ELP) progress, according to NYSESLAT performance trends. • In grades 3–5, writing and speaking were the two weakest modalities, with high percentages of students scoring in the lowest proficiency bands. Additionally, 41% of ELLs exhibit early warning indicators, including underperformance in ELA, low language acquisition gains, and social-emotional disengagement. These factors highlight the need for engaging, affirming, and language-rich opportunities beyond the school day. The Basketball program is designed as a before- and after-school initiative that integrates physical activity with literacy and social-emotional learning. Rooted in our mission to promote high expectations, multilingualism, and rigorous literacy instruction, the program offers students meaningful opportunities to: • Build academic vocabulary and syntactic awareness through thematic content related to sports • Strengthen all four language modalities (reading, writing, listening, speaking) via structured activities such as research projects, oral debates, reflection journals, and team communication drills • Foster community, confidence, and identity through peer collaboration and culturally relevant instruction By connecting literacy with real-world content—basketball—the program engages students who might otherwise be disengaged, while also reinforcing cross-disciplinary skills that translate to ELA and content-area classrooms. The integration of ENL pedagogy with sports ensures that students develop language both formally and informally in rich, communicative environments. Data Sources Used • Spring 2024 NYSESLAT Modality and Proficiency Reports: Identifies listening, writing, and speaking as key areas of need in grades 3–5 • EDAT Early Warning Indicators: Shows high concentration of newcomers and at-risk ELLs (41%) • Demographic Overview in EDAT: Reveals dominance of Chinese and Spanish home languages, indicating a need for scaffolded instruction that bridges cultural and linguistic assets • Progress Monitoring and ELP Growth Data: Highlights limited gains in language proficiency and the need for additional supports beyond the core day This initiative serves not only as an academic intervention, but as a vehicle for empowerment, belonging, and voice —a reflection of our school’s commitment to nurturing confident, multilingual learners ready to lead, advocate, and thrive.
