Strengthening Explicitly Literacy Instruction for our ELLs: P.S. 94K is committed to building teacher capacity through high-quality, research-based professional development that directly addresses the academic and linguistic needs of our multilingual learners. In alignment with our instructional foci and our continued pursuit of rigorous, affirming, and differentiated instruction, we will prioritize the training of our ENL teachers in the Edwards Orton-Gillingham (EOG) methodology. Our Title III-funded professional development plan will focus on equipping ENL and bilingual educators with the tools necessary to deliver explicit, structured, multisensory foundational skills and phonics instruction to English Language Learners (ELLs) at all levels of language proficiency and across all elementary grade bands. The EOG methodology is uniquely suited to address the needs of multilingual learners as it supports students in systematically mastering the structure of the English language—while simultaneously engaging all four modalities: listening, speaking, reading, and writing. This is especially critical at P.S. 94K, where 51.7% of our students are ELLs, and 85.3% are classified as newcomers (per EDAT 2024). Additionally, NYSESLAT modality analysis shows that our ELLs continue to underperform in speaking and writing, reinforcing the need for direct, structured language DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 275 of 304 instruction that targets the productive domains. Professional Development Activities: Additionally, We will be partnering with Edwards-Orton Gillingham to elevate the instruction of foundational skills, phonics and ELA literacy skills over all with a methodology proven to be aligned to the Science of Reading. This professional development plan directly advances our Instructional Focus by: • Strengthening our ENL team’s ability to design explicit, differentiated, small group instruction rooted in language and academic data • Supporting teachers in tailoring instruction through ongoing progress monitoring • Equipping educators with research-based strategies to improve student achievement in literacy, language development, and content-area access It also aligns with our SEL Integration Focus by embedding consistent, confidence-building routines that reinforce emotional regulation, empathy, and collaborative learning—essential elements of both the EOG framework and successful language development. Through this investment, we will ensure that every ENL teacher at P.S. 94K is prepared to meet our multilingual learners wherever they are in their journey—academically, linguistically, and emotionally. This is not just a training— it is a long-term commitment to inclusive, affirming, and high-impact instruction that empowers students to thrive. We will partner directly with Edwards Orton-Gillingham (EOG) to provide the following two-tiered training plan: • Level 1 Training: Focused on the fundamentals of structured literacy, including phonemic awareness, alphabetic principle, multisensory instruction, and core routines to support decoding, encoding, and early reading comprehension. Teachers will learn how to deliver direct and scaffolded instruction tailored to the linguistic needs of Entering and Emerging ELLs. • Level 2 Training: Designed to deepen instructional practice by equipping teachers with strategies for advancing orthographic mapping, morphology, vocabulary development, syntax, and fluency. This level will prepare teachers to differentiate instruction for Transitioning, Expanding, and Commanding level students and provide targeted support aligned with their Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD). Both levels will include embedded coaching and application tasks where teachers: • Analyze student language and literacy data to inform instruction • Design and deliver skill-based small group instruction tailored to language acquisition levels • Implement multisensory routines that integrate SEL and foster confidence in multilingual learners • Develop lesson plans that address all four language modalities using the EOG framework Professional development will be provided in a hybrid format to accommodate teachers’ schedules and will take place both after school (via per-session) and during designated professional learning days. Funding and Compliance with Title III ELL Regulations: • This EOG training will be funded in part by Title III ELL allocations. Vendor costs (EOG) will be calculated as part of OTPS and will remain under the 20% threshold of the total allocation. • Teacher per-session compensation for attending afterschool training sessions will also be funded through Title III ELL and will not exceed 10% of the total allocation. • Materials, digital licenses, and follow-up coaching sessions included in the partnership will directly support the implementation of small group instruction and progress monitoring, consistent with our school’s instructional focus. For each activity, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis (e.g., patterns and trends identified) that support the need for the professional development, and DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 276 of 304 Data sources (e.g., school survey teacher responses, in-house teacher surveys, teacher observation data) used to justify the professional development. Teachers will work on connecting literacy to the arts: With the assistance of our ENL department, our teachers will work on developing scaffolds and supports for our English language learners while learning theater. Strengthening Explicitly Literacy Instruction for our ELLs: The professional development we are proposing under Title III ELL funding is rooted in our school’s core commitment to advancing equitable, rigorous, and affirming instruction for our multilingual learners. Specifically, our rationale for training our ENL and bilingual teachers in the Edwards Orton-Gillingham (EOG) methodology emerges from both quantitative performance data and qualitative instructional trends that point to persistent challenges in foundational literacy and oral language development for our ELL students. Our teachers consistently identify a lack of cohesive training in how to support ELLs with foundational literacy— particularly phonics, phonemic awareness, and word recognition—which impacts their ability to deliver targeted small group instruction aligned to students’ linguistic needs and Zones of Proximal Development (ZPD). This gap became even more evident through: • In-house instructional walkthroughs and formal observations, where ENL teachers expressed uncertainty about how to explicitly scaffold phonics instruction. • Teacher feedback from our Spring 2025 Professional Learning Survey, which highlighted the need for a systematic approach to teaching reading that supports decoding, encoding, fluency, and vocabulary development across language proficiency levels. The Edwards Orton-Gillingham (EOG) methodology directly addresses these gaps. It offers a structured, multisensory, and sequential approach to foundational literacy—designed to support diverse learners, including ELLs. By training teachers in both Level 1 and Level 2 EOG, we are: • Ensuring they are equipped to teach ELLs across all grade levels, from initial phonemic awareness to advanced morphological understanding. • Enabling teachers to embed language development across all four modalities—reading, writing, listening, and speaking—through consistent, affirming literacy routines. • Supporting our instructional focus of targeted small group instruction that is explicit, differentiated, and data- responsive. This professional learning also aligns with our SEL Integration Focus. EOG routines promote student confidence, perseverance, and ownership of learning—fostering emotional regulation and resilience as students experience success with previously challenging skills. This approach strengthens our school-wide efforts to reduce behavioral incidents and increase engagement through emotionally supportive, culturally sustaining instruction. Training ENL teachers in EOG is not simply a literacy intervention; it is a strategic, mission-aligned investment in our multilingual learners. It is supported by clear trends in performance data, teacher feedback, and classroom observations. It reinforces P.S. 94K’s commitment to ensuring all students—regardless of background or proficiency level—have access to high-quality, rigorous, and emotionally supportive instruction that allows them to thrive. Data Sources Used to Justify Professional Development • 2024 NYSESLAT Modality Data: Revealed consistent underperformance in writing and speaking, especially among Entering and Emerging ELLs. • ELL Data Analysis Tool (EDAT): Showed high numbers of newcomers and early warning indicators requiring foundational supports. • In-house Teacher Survey Data (Spring 2025): Indicated a strong need for phonics-specific training tailored to ELLs. • Classroom Observations and Coaching Notes: Documented limited use of structured, multisensory phonics instruction during ENL and small group time. Strengthening Explicitly Literacy Instruction for our ELLs: ENL teachers, Special Education Teachers and Dual Language teachers serving our ELLs Students Asynchronous Level 1: Self-Paced Level 1 Training, accredited by International Dyslexia Association, aligns with the Structured Literacy Knowledge and Practice Standards. Participants will engage in a series of modules designed to equip them with the essential skills for teaching reading and spelling using the structured, multi-sensory Orton-Gillingham approach. Through detailed lessons, hands-on activities, and guided practice, participants will gain mastery over phonological awareness, multi-sensory instruction, lesson planning and many more structured literacy practices. Engaging, Interactive Learning: Dive into dynamic, self-paced modules packed with practical strategies and multisensory techniques you can apply immediately. Practical Tools for Real Results: Learn how to implement structured literacy effectively, making a measurable difference in your students' success. Expert-Guided Content: Build your skills with evidence-based methods aligned with the International Dyslexia Association’s Knowledge and Practice Standards. Level 2: The Level 2 training course equips trainees with the skills and knowledge to effectively implement the Orton-Gillingham Approach in one-on-one tutoring, as well as small group and classroom settings. This training dives deeper into understanding the dyslexic profile, exploring the neuropsychological evaluation process, and using assessment data to inform instruction. It also provides guidance on building the application portfolio required for certification. Trainees at this level are encouraged to pursue a supervised practicum and consider applying for certification through The International Dyslexia Association (Structured Literacy Dyslexia Interventionist (C-SLDI)) and the Orton- Gillingham Academy (Associate Level). DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 285 of 304 Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Immigrant Outline Title III Application for Immigrant Funding REQUIREMENTS Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds can be used for activities that provide enhanced instructional opportunities for immigrant students, which include: Identification and acquisition of curricular materials, educational software and technologies to be used in the program. Family literacy, parent outreach and training activities designed to assist parents in becoming active participants in the education of their children and understand their rights and the benefits of multilingualism. Support for personnel, including teacher aides, parent coordinators, and school counselors who have been specifically trained, or are being trained, to provide services to immigrant students and families. Provision of tutorials, mentoring, and academic or career counseling for immigrant students, as well as engagement in pre-college and work-based learning experiences. Basic instructional services directly attributable to the aforementioned school districts, including costs for additional classroom supplies, transportation costs, or other costs directly attributable to such services. Other instructional services that are designed to assist immigrant students to achieve in elementary schools and secondary schools in the United States, such as programs of introduction to the educational system and civics education. School activities, coordinated with community-based organizations, institutions of higher education, private sector entities, or other entities with expertise in working with immigrants, to assist families of immigrant students by offering comprehensive community services. Funding follows similar guidelines for Title III funds established by the Office of Multilingual Learners (OML) and can only be used to provide supplementary services to immigrant students, who may or may not be English Language Learners (ELLs). Please note: Immigrant students are not necessarily English Language Learners (ELLs), and ELLs may or may not be immigrants. For the purpose of this funding, immigrant students are defined as individuals who were not born in any U.S. state (this includes the District of Columbia and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico); and have not been attending one or more schools in any one or more states for more than three full, academic years (the months need not be consecutive). DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 286 of 304 Priority areas for the use of Title III, Part A - Immigrant Funds are as follows: Extended day programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to provide targeted, small-group instruction, credit recovery support, and postsecondary preparation. Implementing strong student supports to increase graduation rates. Community-based and school-based programming (e.g., before school, after school, Saturday program) to strengthen partnerships between immigrant families and schools. Developing new and/or enhancing existing programs for newcomers (meeting criteria defined above). Supplementing the opening or growth of Transitional Bilingual Education programs and/or Dual Language Bilingual Education programs serving immigrant students. Professional learning on meeting the needs of immigrant students, creating a welcoming and inclusive school environment, and effective instructional practices. For more information on Title III requirements, refer to NYSED’s guidance on allowable and unallowable expenditures for Title III. For specific allocation amounts per school, see the School Allocation Memo or contact your ML/ELL Director or ELL Compliance and Performance Specialist. DIRECTIONS Please complete the application below. Schools must use the funds to support their immigrant students by developing a plan that has each of the following components: Direct instruction, supplemental program for immigrant students, Professional development that bolsters the instructional practice of teachers of immigrant students, and Family engagement activities that support families of immigrant students. Completed applications must be submitted in iPlan for review and approval. Please refer to the respective School Allocation Memo. Schools are not permitted to use funds until they submit a plan and receive notification of its approval. Newcomer Reading Intervention and Orientation Program for Newcomer ELLs: 8 Teachers. For students in grades k , 1, and 2 the sessions will take place during the afternoon. It will be and afterschool program. This program will consist of 20 teaching sessions after school, delivered twice a week (Wednesdays and Thursdays), starting the week of December 15, 2025, and concluding the week of March 26, 2025. For students in grades 3, 4 and 5 This program will consist of 9 teaching sessions after school, delivered once a week (Saturdays), starting the week of December 13, 2025, and concluding the week of March 23, 2026. DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 289 of 304 The program is designed to support newcomer immigrant students, specifically ELLs as per their Language Assessment data (NYSITELL or NYSESLAT). The goal is to improve literacy development in foundational reading skills, vocabulary acquisition, and comprehension, while also addressing social-emotional needs through cultural orientation to life and school routines in New York City. Teachers will provide direct instruction to the same group of students during each session. Activities will include: - Reading Skills: Phonemic awareness, decoding strategies, high-frequency word acquisition, and vocabulary development. - Comprehension Strategies: Self-monitoring, fluency-building activities, and reading responses aligned with the science of reading and NYC Reads initiatives. - Cultural Orientation: Lessons will focus on NYC public transportation, school routines, community resources, and cultural norms to support social integration. All instructional materials, including texts, vocabulary cards, and NYC-specific resources, will be purchased with Title III Immigrant funds. Each student will maintain a portfolio of their progress, including formative assessments, reading logs, and cultural orientation activities. Students will be grouped by their language proficiency and grade level and grade, based on NYSITELL and classroom formative assessments. - Number of Groups: 8 groups - Student-to-Teacher Ratio: 1 teacher per group with 6-10 students Each teacher will work with the same group of students throughout the program to ensure continuity and build strong instructional relationships. All sessions will be conducted in person. In case of a school closure, sessions will transition to remote learning to ensure program continuity. The primary language of instruction will be English, with strategic scaffolding in students’ home languages to support comprehension as needed. The program will be supervised by the Assistant Principal, who will monitor lesson planning, student progress, and alignment with Title III Immigrant objectives. Attendance will be recorded at each session and documented in teacher binders and the AP’s binder. Parents will be notified about the program in both English and their preferred languages. Permission slips for participation will be sent home in multiple languages to ensure accessibility and understanding. This program is designed to address the unique needs of newcomer ELLs by providing targeted literacy instruction and cultural orientation, helping students succeed academically while adapting to their new environment. Rationale for Title III Immigrant Funded Programs Describe the rationale for each Title III Immigrant funded program: For each program, include: Qualitative and quantitative data analysis that support the need for program, and Data sources used to justify the need for the program The Newcomer Reading Intervention and Orientation Program was developed in response to the following data- driven insights: Quantitative Data: - NYSITELL: Assessments of the 77 newcomer ELL students show that the majority are at the Entering DBN: 15K094 School: P.S. 094 The Henry Longfellow 290 of 304 proficiency level, indicating significant gaps in foundational reading skills, vocabulary acquisition, and comprehension. - Classroom Formative Assessments: Data from exit tickets, reading logs, and quizzes highlight that newcomer students in need of support in decoding words, building fluency, and understanding grade-level texts. These findings reflect a critical need for targeted intervention. - Attendance and Participation: Observations of participation and engagement in classroom discussions, along with classroom work, suggest the needs in navigating academic and cultural expectations. - 1st Enter NYC Date Data Qualitative Data: - Teacher Observations: Teachers shared observations the needs of supporting newcomer students in accessing content, participating in classroom activities, and developing the confidence needed to succeed academically. - Parent Feedback: During school-based parent meetings and conferences, parents shared hopes supporting their children to adjust to the school environment, communicate effectively, and complete schoolwork. - Student Interviews: Discussions with newcomer students revealed needs in navigating school routines, exploring NYC, understanding classroom expectations, and engaging with peers. Data Sources Used to Justify the Program - NYSITELL: This provide baseline data on students’ English language proficiency levels and highlight the need for targeted literacy intervention. - 1st Enter NYC Date - Formative Assessments: Reading logs, exit tickets, and teacher-generated quizzes indicate specific areas where students require additional support in literacy. - Grade-level meeting notes and informal teacher surveys highlight the needs of language development and cultural adjustment for newcomer students. - Input from parents gathered during family engagement events. - Insights from conversations with students. Please make sure to describe: Professional development to be provided Activities that teachers will engage with by topic Whether the professional development provided will be at cost to Title III Immigrant funds Our Journey at P.S.94 has been a transformation for change. Our 2024-2025 ELA and NYSESLAT data indicated that our students needed additional scaffold support and differentiation in reading, specifically when unpacking unfamiliar words. In addition, we also needed to provide opportunities for students to develop their oral language skills. PS 94 researched an approach that would be inclusive and focused on supporting language. We will continue to learn and Implement the Sheltered Instruction Observation Protocol (SIOP) during the first and second grade reading Intervention program as we believe this is the most effective way to incorporate the strongest elements of reading. SIOP language methodologies will be integrated during guided reading and small group Instruction for first and second grade reading intervention student. Ultimately, the integration of the SIOP model will provide multiple pathways for students to participate and succeed academically with additional literacy language support in itemized knowledge, early reading strategies, decoding strategies, self-correction strategies monitoring reading strategies, using all sources of Information to read, vocabulary development, fluency in an effort to improve reading comprehension using multiple sources of tens. Teachers will receive professional development to understand how to best support our English language learners and will be required to attend training sessions on Mondays from 2:20-3:20 p.m. Professional development will include the understanding of the science of reading and explicit instruction to target the needs of English Language Learners. Teachers will also receive professional development sessions where they will focus on unpacking data and designing skill based differentiated instruction. Additionally this school year, we are teaching phonics and morphology using the Edwards-Orton Gillingham approach which is a more effective and explicit way to teach students how the English language works. All agendas and attendance sheets will be kept in PS 94 Professional Development Binder. These sessions will be conducted at no cost to Title Ill. Records to our meetings will be maintained by Assistant Principal Rose Rodriguez. If the sessions are conducted in person, Ms. Rodriguez will keep all handouts, agendas and signing sheets in a binder. If sessions are conducted digitally, Ms. Rodriguez will print out the digital attendance (google form or zoom attendance tool) as well as all resources and agendas shared during the meeting. PS 94 recognizes that parents are our children’s first teachers. Research shows that students who have involved parents outperform their peers academically. We are proud that our parents are active participants in the education or their child. Parents participate in the first Friday of every month, which they visit their child’s classroom and work on a special project, read or play math games and learn about language strategies to support our ELLs. In addition to the scheduled parent-teacher conferences, teachers meet with parents individually during their parent engagement block every Tuesday or every day of the week for 30 minutes during teachers' office hours while we are following the blended learning model. Staff members will inform main office and safety desk of all scheduled in-person appointments. All social distancing protocols will be followed when a meeting is scheduled to be conducted in- person. Assistant Principal Rodriguez uses the EDAT tool (when available) to identify students at risk and uses that Information to meet with parents and teachers to discuss ways to support those students' language development. When not available, Assistant Principal Rodriguez uses the RLAT to identify students at risk and discuss support with their parents and collaborating teachers. Teachers and Assistant Principals offer workshops to parents to get a deeper understanding of our goals, our students' language development, the different proficiency levels, and ways to support language development for our Ells at home. Parents can attend workshops in their preferred language along with information and resources about the workshop. All workshops are planned to be conducted in-person. Parents will be notified of the workshop through our school's monthly calendars as well our teachers' monthly newsletter to parents. Calendars and newsletters are sent home in the parents preferred language, they are also posted in all our digital platforms. We will also include information about the workshops in our school website, which can be translated to our parents' preferred language. Records of the agendas, attendance and handouts will be maintained in the parent communication binder in the main office. Copies of agendas, attendance, handouts and exit slips will be maintained in Ms. Gonzalez's parent workshop binder as well. Workshops will be conducted in English, Spanish and Chinese as we will have translators and FM units available for parents to use and participate together during the workshop. These sessions will be conducted at no cost to Title Ill. Activities are for parents of immigrant students, both ELLs and non-Ells.
